#6987. The 10 Leather Jackets You (Yes, You) Could Actually Pull Off

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

All you need is the right style, and you’re the coolest guy at the bar.

I bought my first leather jacket at a vintage fair in an old English cathedral. I was 19, and a middle-aged British lady told me I looked good in it. Clearly a sales technique, but it worked, and I shelled out my grocery money for it. I had grand plans of fixing up my dad’s old Honda the following semester and riding around campus in a sick jacket. For various reasons, that didn’t end up happening, and I ended up on a 50cc Yamaha Zuma, a fun little scooter but one where a creaky moto jacket looked more like a comic prop than something out of a Dennis Hopper film. C’est la vie; we all look a little ridiculous in our 20s.

Despite what cinema tells you, you don’t need to ride a motorcycle in order to wear a leather jacket. Or fly a WWII-era fighter plane. Or be Steve McQueen photographed for Esquire. Leather jackets are imbued with the undeniable cool factor of their original functionality—like work jackets, chore coats, and waxed canvas, the modern menswear landscape supports multiple usages. From happy hours to midwestern winters to, yes, actually riding a motorcycle, we’ve rounded up nine Esquire-approved picks to guide your foray into the iconic outerwear. And to come full circle, I did actually test some of these on a motorcycle. More than a decade later, I finally got my license and am fixing up that old Honda; thank God for YouTube.

Leather jackets can be tricky to get right. Too trend focused and it’s a thousand-dollar flash in the pan; too trend agnostic and you get a museum artifact rather than a wearable piece. Ayr nailed the balance on its cleverly named Scrambler jacket, which draws on moto history and sits in the Goldilocks zone between classic and contemporary.

I’ve worn it motorcycling through the Appalachian mountain roads and plan on packing it for an upcoming jaunt to Europe. If you’re looking for a more classic moto style, I also own and highly endorse the elegantly named Baby Grand. The brand (and menswear lead Brice Pattison) has the Midas touch of outerwear; every style is worth its weight in gold.

Sure, there are excellent (and expensive) picks from Loewe, Celine, and Tom Ford with extra-premium materials, brand-specific takes on classic silhouettes, and the extra designer oomph that makes them worth it in some circles. But in this price range, I’m more interested in the experimental and new perspective of a brand like Stòffa, which you can tell is having fun with fabric and silhouettes. This Wave Leather jacket somehow manages to be sleek and drape-y all at once. A daydream of a jacket that’s definitely pricey but somehow still more affordable by half than other designer pieces out there.

Budget leather goods can be problematic. Under a certain price threshold, you’re almost certainly not getting the real thing, in which case you’d be better off going with a disclosed imitation-leather piece from the likes of all-star mall brands like Banana Republic and Abercrombie & Fitch. Or eBay, where a tape measure and a search for Wilson’s Leather or vintage Orvis will unlock a treasure trove of perfectly patinated jackets.

For brand-new on a budget, I recommend the Jacket Maker. Its cut will be a little less forgiving than those of the pricier brands in this list, but if you’re a guy who fits well into off-the-rack suits, you shouldn’t have an issue. Plus, the brand offers a custom, made-to-measure jacket-design tool that has me tempted to re-create McQueen’s from The Great Escape.

This is the one on this list that takes the most bravado. This is the one that upon first wear will get you some looks for your usual drinking buddies. And with good reason—a Schott Perfecto is a loaded garment. Marlon Brando, James Dean, the Ramones, and any cool guy that’s worn one before are not easy acts to follow. But if this is what the heart wants, we stand behind you fully.

The key is in how you wear it. Crucially, you should not “style” it. You should pick it up as much as possible and wear it with as many disparate collections of clothes as you can. For the first few weeks, you might be seen as “The guy trying to wear a leather jacket.” But after a month or two, that will turn into “The leather jacket guy.” Wear it hard, wear it proud, and wear it everywhere.

And one note on the type of leather. If you’re buying new, we think you should go for horsehide. It’s the toughest of the bunch and takes the longest to mold to your body, but that makes it fun.

A shearling jacket is hard to pull off in day-to-day life. Done incorrectly, it can quickly devolve into a kind of cosplay of a WWII fighter pilot. Overland’s Lance jacket retains the genuine sheepskin lining but softens the look with a handsome dark navy colorway paired with a more subtle brown lining, giving you the warmth and badassery without crossing over into costume territory—unless that’s your thing, in which case the brand’s B-3 bomber re-creation is right up your alley. (Though, as always, Todd Snyder’s take is more wearable.)

I want every leather jacket to have an origin story like Taylor Stitch’s, which begins with one of the brand’s designers re-creating a beloved café-racer-style jacket his dad bought in the ’70s and wore to perfection. It turns out the original manufacturer, Golden Bear, was around the corner from TS, and the two brands worked together to reissue the style.

It’s got some modern twists like a Thinsulate lining but retains the design cues that make it functional on a bike and just plain cool on its own. I’ve coveted this jacket for years and recently got my hands on it IRL, and, boy, it does not disappoint. The Whiskey Steerhide colorway looks a little early-aughts coded online, but in person it looks like the magic liquid itself: golden and invigorating. For a non-insulated, more lifestyle-oriented take on the silhouette, I’d point you to Billy Reid’s Blake jacket, which is my dive bar go-to.

Rough out has the wearability of suede, and doesn’t look as turbo as smooth leather. But unlike typical suede, which splits the original hide, rough out retains the whole of the hide. That makes it just as durable and water-resistant as any other leather.

Add that to Billy Reid’s Blake rider shape, and you get a jacket that any guy—literally any guy, we swear—off the street could toss on and feel good in.

Look, the whole oversized vibe is cool and all—but we’re also keenly aware it doesn’t work for everyone. Sure, it brings that chill, relaxed vibe. But sometimes you can just end up looking like a kid wearing their dad’s clothes. If you’re looking for something that hugs the body a bit more, opt for Mili Lotan’s Hammond jacket. It’s got enough slouch you could wear a few layers beneath, but will flatter the body a little more than some of the other options on this list.

I am big on brands that care about details and let the clothes speak for themselves. Octobre Éditions is one such brand. The cut and the cloth set the clothes apart and create longevity outside the trend cycle. When you’ve designed a perfect OCBD, you can never really go out of style. Same too with the brand’s excellent Larren jacket, which I have and is unfortunately currently OOS. Thankfully, the Peylor cuts the same silhouette in a nubby split leather that I’m a little envious of. Get it while the getting’s good.

This is a dream leather jacket—the sort of thing you’d have on a list of “Things I’d Buy If I Had All The Money In The World.” It’s rugged, it’s suave, and damn is it comfy. Just looking at that sumptuous shearling tells you this is a jacket you’d never want to take off, especially on those brutal winter days.

Our editors spend a lot of time assessing what’s in, what’s cool, and what you should care about. It’s literally part of our whole job. Sometimes that means trying things out first-hand, sometimes it’s about thorough research, and sometimes it’s just something you know. Whatever the means, we’ve done the work to figure out what is and isn’t worth your time and money.

#6982. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6975. How to Read the Dune Book Series in Order

Our foolproof guide to getting caught up before Dune: Part Three hits theaters.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

So you're fired up about Dune: Part Three? The trilogy-capper is shaping up to be one of the biggest movies of the year, costarring just about every A-lister in Hollywood, including Timothée Chalamet, Zendaya, Robert Pattinson, and Florence Pugh. If you want to dive into the world of Frank Herbert's beloved novels before the film’s release on December 18, congratulations! You’ve come to the right place.

Dune is the world's best-selling science fiction novel. It's also considered one of the best books of all time. But whether you've dabbled in Dune lore before or you're completely new to the wild world of Arrakis, there's something for everyone in this Titanic-sized series about power, violence, and fate. You've got an exciting literary journey ahead.

Published in 1965, the book's massive success inspired Herbert to write several sequels, which brought the total number of novels in the series up to six when he died in 1986. The first Dune novel was split in half to cover both of Denis Villeneuve’s films so far, and the third novel, Dune: Messiah, will be adapted in Dune: Part Three. From there? Well, your guess is as good as mine.

Not only did Herbert write four more Dune novels, but his son Brian Herbert continued to write a little under two dozen more books in the Dune franchise following his father’s death. He teamed up with sci-fi writer Kevin J. Anderson to coauthor a trilogy of Dune prequels and then several spin-offs thereafter that filled in the gaps in Dune’s history.

But what's the right way to start your Dune journey? Well, there's no right answer. Some argue that the books should be read in the order of their fictive timeline, while others argue that they should be read in the order of publication.

Here's our advice: Read Frank Herbert's six novels first, then dabble in the spin-offs and prequels however you like. Given that many are grouped into smaller series within the larger story, you can sample bits and pieces of the universe. That's the beauty of Dune—it's a detailed series that rewards completionism, but the entry points are numerous.

Read on for a full breakdown of the books, listed here in the order in which they were published. Happy reading, spiceheads!—Josh Rosenberg

Dune is set far in the future, in an intergalactic feudal society where powerful noble houses fight for control over resources, armies, and planetary power. House Atreides is ordered to take control of Arrakis, a barren desert planet with a brutal climate. Arrakis is the only place to mine melange, a natural resource that produces a psychoactive drug called spice, which allows human beings to unlock their minds. On Arrakis, House Atreides is betrayed by its rival House Harkonnen, which sets off a battle over the valuable planet. The planet itself is inhabited by giant sandworms and a native population known as Fremen, who, over the generations, have learned to survive with water as their most precious resource and currency. When House Atreides scion Paul is targeted as a potential messiah to lead the planet—and galaxy—toward a new era, an epic story of war, betrayal, and mysticism unfolds. Cinephiles, this one is a must: Denis Villeneuve's Dune and Dune: Part Two span this book.

In Herbert's first sequel, Paul Atreides, now known as Muad'Dib, rules the known universe as the most powerful emperor of all time. Worshipped as a messiah by the people of Arrakis, Paul faces enmity from the warring political houses under his control. Is any one ruler meant to have such absolute power? In Dune Messiah, Herbert unmakes his previous novel, all while warning, "No more terrible disaster could befall your people than for them to fall into the hands of a hero." Film fans, don't miss this one—you'll want to be caught up when Villeneuve's Dune Part Three lands in theaters on December 19, 2026.

Children of Dune picks up with Leto and Ghanima Atreides, the twin children of Paul Atreides, nine years after their father's mysterious disappearance into the wastelands of Arrakis. The twins' prophetic abilities are coveted by their manipulative aunt Alia, who rules the Empire, but these two young prophets refuse to be anyone's pawns.

Thirty-five hundred years after the events of Children of Dune, the once-desert planet of Arrakis is now a lush paradise, and Leto Atreides sits on the throne. Millennia ago, Leto merged with a sandworm to grant himself immortality, but the cost to his humanity has been enormous. Can a rebellion led by Siona, a rival relative, unseat this fearsome despot?

Leto Atreides is dead, Arrakis (now called Rakis) is once again a desert wasteland, and the Empire has fallen into ruin. A young girl named Sheeana seems to fulfill a prophecy foretold by the late God Emperor, sending religious fervor through the galaxy. Is Sheeana destined to return the Empire to its former glory?

In Frank Herbert's final Dune novel, Arrakis has been destroyed, and the fate of the Empire rests in the hands of a mysterious matriarchal order known as the Bene Gesserit. On the planet Chapterhouse, the sisters are breeding sandworms and seeking to control spice production, with the goal of remaking the galaxy for a brighter future.

Brian Herbert (Frank Herbert’s son) and Kevin J. Anderson's Prelude to Dune trilogy begins with this story of the generation before the one in Dune—namely, that of Leto Atreides, father of Paul. In House Atreides, we see how Leto's rivalries and relationships sowed the catalyzing events of Dune.

In House Harkonnen, Leto Atreides's longtime rival, the Baron Vladimir Harkonnen, comes into view. We also meet Abulurd Rabban, brother and foil to the baron. Turns out, there are good people in House Harkonnen—who knew?

In House Corrino, Herbert and Anderson conclude their prequel series, bringing the story up to the climactic events set to unfold in Dune. This tapestry of politics, warmongering, and spice battles ends with the birth of Paul Atreides, preparing us for the saga we know and love (and now have already read).

Working from Frank Herbert's notes for a "Dune 7," his son and Kevin J. Anderson again expanded the series in Legends of Dune, a new trilogy. The first installment, The Butlerian Jihad, digs into an event Herbert often referred to but never captured at scale: the long-ago war in which humans fought for their freedom from "the thinking machines." Set 10,000 years before Dune, this volume sees the familiar chess pieces come into view.

The Legends of Dune trilogy continues with The Machine Crusade, set two decades after The Butlerian Jihad. The thinking machines fight back, refusing to go quietly into that good night. Meanwhile, on Arrakis, a band of outlaws take their first steps to becoming the Fremen, a race of people that OG Dune fans know and love.

Check out that sandworm on the cover! The Legends of Dune trilogy concludes with The Battle of Corrin, which tees up a final apocalyptic showdown between humans and robots. Fans of Dune know how this one ends, but it sure is fun to see how Herbert and Anderson get there.

Dune Sequels, a two-volume spin-off series, concludes the storyline from Herbert's six original novels, with insight from a long-lost outline found hidden in one of the author's safety deposit boxes. In Hunters of Dune, we pick up with the escaping fugitives last seen at the end of Chapterhouse: Dune as they strengthen their powers and fight for the future of the human race.

Herbert and Anderson answer more burning questions in this second volume of the Dune Sequels series—namely, the future of Arrakis and the outcome of the war between man and machine.

Set between Dune and Dune Messiah, the Heroes of Dune spin-off series opens with a tale of Paul Atreides. Dune ends with Paul ruling Arrakis, while Dune Messiah opens with him ruling the galaxy. Just how did he gain control of the Empire? Read Paul of Dune to find out.

Heroes of Dune continues with this second and final installment, set after the events of Dune Messiah. The Winds of Dune picks up after Paul Atreides's disappearance into the Arrakis desert, leaving the Empire in crisis and the line of succession in question. Who will hold everything together?

The Great Schools of Dune trilogy opens with Sisterhood of Dune, set almost a century after the game-changing Battle of Corrin. With the thinking machines destroyed, political and religious movements rise, setting the stage for an epic conflict between reason and faith. You'll want to read this one to understand more about HBO's Dune: Prophecy series.

In Book Two of the Schools of Dune Trilogy, the thinking machines are gone, but a new school opens to teach humans the efficient techniques of those machines. The Butlerian jihadists staunchly oppose any machinist way of life, however, and pick a dangerous fight with the Mentat School. What ensues is a showdown for humanity's future, with the risk of a potential dark age.

Want to learn even more about the Bene Gesserit sisterhood from Herbert's original novels? Then Navigators of Dune is the book for you. In this third and final volume of the Great Schools of Dune trilogy, we learn about the origins of the Bene Gesserit and see their secretive way of life develop.

The latest trilogy from Herbert and Anderson, The Caladan Trilogy, begins with The Duke of Caladan, a prequel about the life of Leto Atreides. Just how did the ruler of a quiet planet become such a power player in a galactically fateful story? If that's the question on your mind, this is the book you ought to hit next.

Herbert and Anderson take it all the way back to OG Dune in this prequel about Lady Jessica, mother of Paul Atreides. In The Lady of Caladan, Lady Jessica reckons with her choice to betray the Bene Gesserit, her ancient maternal order, while destiny converges around her husband and son.

Have you read everything up to this point about Paul Atreides but you still can't get enough of the guy? We've got a book for you. The Caladan Trilogy concludes with The Heir of Caladan, a deep dive into the young heir's metamorphosis from teenager to messiah.

Set two years before the events of Dune, Princess of Dune delves into the lives of the two most important women in Paul Atreides's life: his wife, Princess Irulan, and his true love, Chani. Though these characters are often at odds in their separate quests to influence Paul, Princess of Dune draws striking parallels between these brave, ambitious, and embattled young women. Through their expanded backstory, one comes to see the events of Dune in a new light.

Superfans will love this collection of novellas, each one taking place in an under-explored corner of the Dune-verse. Just what was Gurney Halleck up to during his lost years working undercover as an Arrakis smuggler? What's it like to be one of the fearsome Sardaukar? How did Shadout Mapes go from a Fremen freedom fighter to an imperial housekeeper? If you've ever asked these questions, you'll find your answers in Sands of Dune.

The Road to Dune contains short stories, previously unpublished works, and correspondence from the late Frank Herbert that all led to the creation of Dune. Plus, you can read the novella “Spice Planet,” based on a detailed outline left by Frank Herbert.

#6973. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6970. Is The Rings of Power Still Worth Watching?

As The Lord of the Rings prequel enters its third of five planned seasons, let’s admit that Jeff Bezos spent a little too much money making this a reality.

When I was a kid, I was obsessed with something called Mystic Knights of Tir Na Nog. If you weren’t a first-grader in 1998, it was a Fox Kids Saturday morning series about four heroes in medieval Ireland who morphed into armored knights to battle dragons and evil witches. It was Power Rangers by way of Celtic mythology, and it kicked ass. I was a wee bit too young for Hercules and Xena then, and Mystic Knights made sword-and-sorcery stories accessible to my Saturday morning-addled brain. Two years after it went off the air, The Lord of the Rings: The Fellowship of the Ring stormed into theaters.

However unfair, it’s through that spectrum of two extreme ends that I see fantasy television like The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power. I have a standard through which I see this genre in this particular medium. On the big screen, fantasy can feel endless, its scope stretching beyond the horizon. On the small screen, you can feel the ceiling over everyone’s heads. Even Game of Thrones had a coffee cup slip through the veil. Which is why, despite the distant relation to Peter Jackson’s immaculate movies, The Rings of Power is quintessential fantasy TV on par with Mystic Knights. That’s not a bad thing—it’s just perspective.

We’re four years and three seasons (lol) into The Rings of Power’s planned five seasons on Prime Video. After all this time, it still hasn’t won over skeptics. There are long and boring reasons why Tolkien faithful have been averse to the prequel show. They have complaints about pacing, plotting, creative liberties from Tolkien’s textbooks of lore, and general execution. Regrettably, they more often than not get mixed up with old-fashioned racism and misogyny. (Valar forbid a dwarf be a Black woman.) But sometimes the worst people you know have a point: The Rings of Power has been an underwhelming experience. Despite its strong start in season 1, the show simply hasn’t escaped the long, long shadow of Jackson’s cinematic trilogy. (Even Jackson never lived up to his own standards. See: The Hobbit.)

Amid the show’s large ensemble, the series bet many of its chips on Charlie Vickers as Sauron. Though handsome, Vickers’ lack of charisma, middling scriptwriting for his role, and a severe lack of sexual tension with co-star Morfydd Clark (as Galadriel) have sadly made Rings of Power somnambulant. Now in season 3, the chance to course-correct is long past, and the rigor mortis of its missteps have now settled in.

Season 1 had a strong start. Sauron is a hottie, and his early relationship to Galadriel carried with it an air of intrigue and yearning until his mask came off. Maybe there could have been a whole show about their forbidden attraction. (Some fans have already coined “Saurondriel.”) The good girl/bad boy pairing is a tried and true tradition, with a subset of the Star Wars fandom possessing a bizarre obsession with Daisy Ridley’s Rey getting it on with Adam Driver’s Kylo Ren. Who are any of us to deny ourselves this pleasure?

But season 2 didn’t seize on nor improve what was its grace. Their characters were kept separate until the finale, where they reunited on the battlefield. It’s a good scene, to be fair, it’s just maddening that what could have been the whole show was just five minutes, including a bloodless sword fight.

Now here we are in season 3, and it’s more of the same. All of the characters are on a journey across Middle-earth, the story is moving along at a steady, if at times meandering pace, Sauron is quite literally plotting everything from his high tower, and there is a clear endpoint at the end of it all: the forging of the One Ring. As of this writing, the season’s four episodes (available to stream now) suggest we’ll get there in due time. There have been some big-name casualties already, and some impressive set pieces that actually feel like things are intensifying at the right temperatures.

Still, this is Lord of the Rings we’re talking about here. For all the lore and imagination Tolkien spilled onto his pages, his was a story still mired in the age-old tradition of good versus evil. That is Rings of Power too, where even morality comes in shades of stark black and white (mostly) and just enough polished VFX and production design to sell the immersion. One could ask, “What more do you want?” Somehow, it’s still not really enough. Even now, there is still too little energy, too little thrust, too little verve—and yes, still too little sex appeal—to warrant undivided attention. The Rings of Power is mighty pretty to look at on a big 50-inch LED screen. (I’m particularly engrossed anytime we follow Daniel Weyman’s Gandalf, who is always strolling through the most picturesque backdrops.) But it’s also far too easy to get distracted by more exciting things, like your laundry machine timer going off. In other words: The Rings of Power is a television show.

For all the money Jeff Bezos spent on making Rings of Power a reality, it’s still just TV. It’s maybe a cruel, cosmic, and frankly righteous joke that the billionaire’s very clear marching orders to his team—“I want my Game of Thrones,” is how he was quoted—have resulted in precisely that. A fantasy television series. Blame the streaming era for pigeonholing everything of this caliber into a blockbuster with lofty expectations. Not all TV should be appointment viewing. Sometimes a TV show can and should just be what it is: Mystic Knights, with a few extra zeroes.

#6969. Designers Say These Are the Best Colors to Pair With Blue in Any Room

They perfectly complement the rainbow's most versatile hue.

A designer-favorite color to use in the home is beautiful, calming blue. Evoking natural elements like the sea and the sky, this serene hue can make any room feel more relaxed and welcoming, yet also more vibrant and bold, depending on the shades. And if this versatile hue is one you’re thinking about adding to your home, check out these beautiful colors that go with blue, proven by these designer spaces.

No matter your design style, this list of 38 colors that go with blue serves as evidence that the hue is as versatile as a neutral and can elevate any space you use it in. Read on to find your new favorite color scheme here.

To contrast the fanciful chinoiserie wallpaper in this San Francisco apartment, interior designer Caitlin Jones Ghajar and her team went bold and chose a warm yellow for the velvet bed frame. “This particular kind of blue lends itself really well to the vistas that are beyond, so you get some Bay view looks,” she says.

In this Amber Lewis–designed kitchen, the duck egg blue cabinetry is grounded by the rust red and cream floor tile. The hue, Light Blue by Farrow & Ball, can lean almost gray in soft lighting, making it a versatile neutral ready to pair with warm tones.

The deep, black accents in this kitchen add drama and depth to the slate blue cabinetry. The graphic color pairing is modern and masculine, while not trying to compete with the lush nature outdoors. Melissa Anderson of OAD Interiors designed this immersive space in Woodstock, New York.

Blue and yellow is a classic and cheerful color pairing. Elevate the duo by choosing a hue with a slightly greener tone, like a chartreuse. In this guest room by French & French, the cool light blue prevents a too-neon appearance.

Pairing a cool tone with a warm tone is a failsafe approach, and this bedroom by Serena Dugan does it expertly. A golden tan wallpaper softens the vibrant indigo accents on the headboard and window treatments. If you’re feeling bold, throw in some orange for added impact.

When you're unsure of what colors go with blue the best, simply opt for more of the same, as French & French did here. Perfect pattern play is achieved in this lounge through the matching blue and white palettes. A cobalt blue wall ties it all together.

For an impactful palette, add some glitz to your shade of blue. This living room, designed by Suzanne Kasler, utilizes metallics like brass and bronze to elevate the everyday hue to glamorous new heights.

In this Santa Fe home designed by the duo behind French & French, a rich, terra-cotta red balances the vibrant blue tones. To ground the swirling marbled wallpaper on the ceiling, the grand bookcases and coordinating rug (and sneaky chair piping!) incorporate the hue throughout the entire space.

In House Beautiful’s 2024 Whole Home, the guest room is cocooned in a mustard and navy color palette. Blue and yellow primary colors play off one another for a vibrant interior. Use neutral bedding to ground the bright shades.

Pink and blue can often feel like a baby shower or gender reveal, but take inspiration from designer Dane Austin to know how to use them correctly. By combining a pastel with a more vibrant tone, you can elevate the color pairing from juvenile to sophisticated.

Look to nature for hues to use with blue. This chic powder room, complete with a navy blue sink, is swathed in a floral green wallpaper. This color pairing is cheerful and can brighten up a smaller space, especially this one by Liz Carroll, which lacks windows.

Adorned in shades of cobalt and navy, the primary bedroom in our 2024 Whole Home in Asheville, North Carolina, continues the moody color palette with an ochre wallpaper. The hues are simultaneously subdued yet impactful, ideal for a serene night’s sleep.

A spa-like and timeless blue and gray color palette can brighten even the dingiest basements. To ensure nothing looks out of place, keep both hues at the same vibrancy level (e.g., a light blue and a light gray).

To add drama to this teal bedroom, designer Lisa Tharp opted for a totally red upholstered bed frame, and somehow it works. Blue and red are both primary colors, which means they pair well when one starts to lean toward the other’s complement (like the slightly green-toned blue walls).

In this playful kids' bathroom, a bright blue is the focal point color in the space. Looking to the color wheel for a clue, homeowner Ashley Maddox and designer Hilary Walker opted for a burnt orange shower curtain as the accent hue.

Pairing a myriad of cool-toned colors can create a sophisticated palette when done well, like in this Boston apartment. A purple rug complements the blue sectional sofa because they have similar textiles, tones, and brightness levels.

If you’re a fan of the blue and yellow primary pair, you’ll love this elevated option. Pairing a deep, rich blue, like this navy kitchen, with a metallic brass accent is a great way to add some glamour and texture to your space.

Monochrome color palettes can be really impactful when done with various shades of the same hue. Take this living room by Mark D. Sikes for example. The deep blue upholstery on the sofa and ottoman ground the lighter blue used to color drench the walls and ceiling.

Avery Cox of Avery Cox Design used a tonal blue for the wall to give this room an immersive effect and paired it with pops of bubblegum pink, purple, and citron yellow. The colors are the perfect pick-me-up when you're lounging indoors on a rainy day.

This robin's egg blue wallpaper is speckled with ivory and forest green to make daydreaming easy. The ivory mirror and green accessories designer Shannon Eddings added to this dining room are the supporting characters that soften the blue so it doesn't overwhelm the space.

#6968. Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6967. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6965. Boots, Bags, and All the New Launches Esquire’s Editors Loved This Week

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Plus an incredible Filson and Baracuta collab.

This is odd to say, but new releases rarely hit at the right time for me. What I mean is, new things typically get dropped early season. Or they are announced on a runway half a year before they ever come out. I never see a new clothing release—I guess it’s more common in home and tech—where I say: I need this right now.

But it happened this week. The first two picks on this list are really calling after my wallet. First, Bennett Winch (a favorite around here) put out its newest suede, this classy navy. #6962. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6960. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.

#6956. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6954. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

#6951. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6950. It's Official: Robert Pattinson is the Next James Bond

After months of speculation and a long race, the next 007 has finally been announced.

He was a late addition to the rumor mill, but Robert Pattinson has officially landed the role of James Bond.

While industry insiders had previously claimed it was a three-way race between Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor and Callum Turner, on Friday, November 13, Puck’s Matthew Belloni revealed that Pattinson was “studio Amazon MGM’s preferred choice for the role.” The other lads were apparently out, and despite the studio requiring the next 007 to commit to a four-Bond-film contract—Pattinson’s contract called for a three-film deal—he has been cast as the iconic secret agent.

“James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime,” Pattinson said in a statement. “I’m deeply grateful to Denis, Amy, David, Tanya, and everyone at Amazon MGM Studios for their belief in me. To say this role is a dream come true is an understatement. I couldn’t be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team.”

Pattinson, born in Barnes, London, in 1986, has a wealth of experience behind him. After an early breakthrough as Cedric Diggory in 2005’s Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire, he became a global teenage heartthrob as vampire Edward Cullen in the hugely successful Twilight franchise. Based on the bestselling books of the same name, the series ran across five films from 2008 to 2012.

Pattinson went on to take a series of more esoteric indie roles, including in David Cronenberg’s Maps to the Stars in 2014—his second film with the director, after 2012’s Cosmopolis—and Robert Eggers’ The Lighthouse in 2019.

He also caught the attention of one of Hollywood’s most lauded directors, Christopher Nolan, and starred as Neil in 2020’s Tenet. Pattinson then took on another major franchise role as the Caped Crusader in Matt Reeves’ 2022 film The Batman. The sequel is due out in 2028.

Meanwhile, Pattinson also moved into the A24 realm, starring in Marty Supreme, alongside Timothée Chalamet, and then The Drama, acting opposite Zendaya. He also starred with her in his second Nolan film, The Odyssey, in which he played the villain Antinous, which has picked up Oscar buzz. Then there's his recent acclaimed performance in Primetime, playing To Catch a Predator host Chris Hansen.

As a result, Pattinson has become one of the industry’s most in-demand and most celebrated actors. Some critics might have argued that it was impossible for one actor to be both Batman and Bond—to front up two of the most legendary roles in pop culture—but the appointment of Pattinson proves that the studio bosses have no doubt that he can make the distinct transition and that he’s the best person to breathe new life into the franchise.

Interestingly, back in 2012, Pattinson said he would “definitely like to go for Bond,” but wanted to wait around twenty years. “There’d be nothing worse than, like, ‘Let’s get a fresh-faced Bond!’” he added, according to NDTV. “That would be the worst idea in the world. It would be ridiculous to reinvent it as some young posh kid.”

Pattinson will take over from Daniel Craig—who was in five films from 2006 to 2021—making him the seventh official Bond. The as-yet-untitled Bond 26 will be produced by David Heyman and Amy Pascal, while Dune’s Denis Villeneuve will direct and Peaky Blinders’ Steven Knight is writing the script. Pattinson will make his debut as the secret service agent when the film is expected to be released, which at this moment is said to be 2028.
