#6949. These Are the Home Trends Gaining Ground for 2027, According to Zillow

A new analysis of millions of for-sale listings reveals the features sellers are increasingly highlighting.

Above: A color-drenched den on Long Island, New York, designed by General Assembly.

Homes are getting smarter, but sellers are also advertising places to unplug. Zillow’s 2027 Home Trends Report finds growing mentions of both AI and tech-free spaces, along with color-drenched rooms, home offices, and “catios.”

The report tracks year-over-year changes in mentions of hundreds of features across millions of for-sale listings. The results reflect how sellers and agents are marketing homes—not a survey of buyers or evidence that a particular feature will help a property sell. Still, they offer a look at the amenities and design details increasingly making their way into listing descriptions. Here are the main takeaways.

Mentions of AI are up 119 percent, the largest increase in this year’s report. Zillow points to AI-powered appliances among the features sellers are advertising, while mentions of app-controlled home systems rose 34 percent.

Spaces to unplug are also appearing 34 percent more often. The report describes an interest in both technology that simplifies daily tasks and designated places to take a break from it.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert, in a statement. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Sellers and their real estate agents are now highlighting spaces to unplug.”

Color-drenched spaces—rooms where the same color extends across the walls, ceiling, and trim—are up 84 percent in listing mentions, making them one of the report’s biggest increases. Zillow sees the approach as part of a move toward bolder, more polished interiors. Exterior finishes are gaining ground, too: painted brick is up 27 percent, and board-and-batten siding rose 24 percent.

Color drenching takes a shade beyond the walls and onto the ceiling, trim, and other architectural details. The approach has even spawned a bathroom variation: tile drenching, which extends the same tile across surfaces for a continuous look.

The language used to describe these homes is changing alongside the features. Mentions of “elevated” rose 71 percent, while “cozy” fell 22 percent, “eclectic” dropped 19 percent, and “boho” declined 13 percent. Cozy remains one of the most common words in listings, but it’s appearing less frequently than it did a year ago.

Kitchen language is moving in the other direction. Mentions of “chef’s kitchens” fell 21 percent, and “gourmet kitchens” declined 15 percent.

Mentions of multigenerational living jumped 58 percent. Zillow connects the increase to homes accommodating the “sandwich generation,” or people caring for children and older family members.

Features that can serve different ages and needs are also appearing more frequently. Grab bars are up 18 percent, wider doorways are up 13 percent, and nurseries rose 9 percent. The report places these changes alongside the growing emphasis on flexible rooms and spaces with more than one use.

The home spa is changing. Mentions of spa-inspired bathrooms rose 60 percent, and cold plunge pools increased 44 percent. Jacuzzis and jetted tubs, meanwhile, each fell about 9 percent.

Spaces for exercise are also gaining ground. Home gyms are up 18 percent, while golf simulators rose 29 percent. Together, the findings cover both workout spaces and amenities marketed around rest and recovery, rather than a single version of the home wellness room.

Cats get a category of their own this year. “Catios”—screened-in outdoor spaces that let indoor cats spend time outside—are appearing in 32 percent more listings. A place to get some air, watch the birds, and keep an eye on the neighbors—the cat has a wish list, too.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6948. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

#6945. John Mulaney’s Roast of New York Is an Instant SNL All-Timer

Veronika Slowikowska has some serious pipes, by the way.

It’s been an incredible year in New York City. The start of Zohran Mamdani’s mayoralship set a joyful tone that underscored the city’s immaculate vibe these last few months: the Knicks’ championship win, Harry Styles’ MSG residency, and a Saturday Halloween/Sunday marathon double whammy. So leave it to John Mulaney to take the city down a peg.

In the latest Saturday Night Live, the sketch of the night was easily “Central Park Horse & Carriage Ride.” Mulaney plays an off-kilter horse-cab driver (“You just trust me implicitly? You realize I have a top hat and a whip, right?”) who shows two tourists, played by Kam Patterson and Marcello Hernández, the best and worst of New York. With the impending ban on horse-drawn carriages in Central Park slated for June 2028, this is among the last times anyone can tour the city like this.

The sketch is unofficially a tribute to Broadway—a New York cultural staple—with short songs that pay homage to shows like Little Shop of Horrors, Funny Girl, Annie Get Your Gun, and West Side Story. The buzzy Oh, Mary! gets a major tip of the hat too, with current star Bowen Yang making a surprise return to 30 Rockefeller Plaza.

After a mandatory number pointing out that Central Park isn’t safe at night, you get Kenan Thompson as a weed cloud, Tommy Brennan as a rival pedicab cyclist (“I’ll take you to the MoMA/Put you in a coma” is a bar, I’m sorry), and a genuine crowd-pleasing duet with James Austin Johnson and Jeremy Culhane who sing “about a boy named Mamdani.” And there’s Yang, as “Mary Todd Lincoln Tunnel,” who sings a cover of “And I Am Telling You” about commuting through New Jersey.

But really, the biggest showstopper of the sketch and frankly all of Saturday Night Live this week is VERONIKA SLOWIKOWSKA. As a marathon runner who still hasn’t finished the race, Slowikowska shows off her golden pipes in a cover/parody of “Don’t Rain on My Parade.” Plainly, she crushes it. It’s moments like these that make you remember that SNL is full of overqualified, overly energetic theater kids now, and not stand-up comics blitzed out on cocaine.

You can watch the sketch in the embed below.

The sketch was maybe the best part of a pretty standout SNL, which was owed to the simple fact that Mulaney was the host. A former show writer turned seven-time guest host, Mulaney’s presence was essentially permission to let everyone’s hair down so they could take bigger swings. Mulaney himself said it: “I used to be a writer at this show, and one of the only difficult things about working here was dealing with the host. So it’s fun when I host, because it’s like there’s no host.” Kinda explains the sketch.

#6943. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6941. King Charles Had the Most Unexpected Response to Robert Pattinson Being Announced as James Bond

The royals have long been fans of the franchise.

#6936. Timothy Dalton's 007 Advice Is the Only Guidance Robert Pattinson Should Follow

The veteran actor notes that the true danger for the super-spy is trying to please everyone.

James Bond’s greatest weakness is not megalomaniacal madmen bent on world domination. Those he can handle. The superspy is far more likely to be taken down by well-meaning committees.

That’s why Robert Pattinson’s casting as filmmaker Denis Villeneuve’s new 007 is a bold and promising choice. The two of them together have amassed enough industry clout to craft the character their own way, to make him distinctive and daring in a way, hopefully, that we haven’t seen before.

Some things shouldn’t change, like his seductiveness, the Walther PPK, and the way he likes his martinis to be shaken. But those things only make up the silhouette of James Bond. Each actor must put his own imprint on the core of Ian Fleming’s debonair secret agent, just as the author did in his original novels. Sean Connery added ruggedness to the savoir faire. George Lazenby gave him a soulful vulnerability in his solo outing as the character. Roger Moore sharpened 007’s sense of humor. Each actor leaves his fingerprints behind as he hands it off to the next.

This leads to the essential question for anyone signing up to play James Bond: Are you strong enough to stand out?

It’s not so easily answered. I’ll let Timothy Dalton, my personal favorite Bond, explain: “I have some good friends from the Bond days, some really good people, but they’re trapped. Everyone’s trapped by it in a way,” he told me. “Whoever does it has got to have a very clear sense of what they want to achieve, because everybody in the world has got a different opinion about it.”

To figure out what comes next, we should look back at what came before.

Dalton (who happens to be Christopher Nolan’s favorite Bond too) made that comment to me back in February 2025, when Amazon MGM Studios first acquired the rights to the 007 character.

When he first took on the role in 1987’s The Living Daylights, his choice was to add a renegade wolf quality. But he also recognized that James Bond must remain, ultimately, the good guy, and that movie was entirely defined by an act of mercy. Fleming’s short story “The Living Daylights” makes up the film’s opening scene: Bond has been sent to take out an enemy sniper, but when he sees that his target is a beautiful cellist (played by Maryam d’Abo), he aims his own rifle to distract her rather than kill her.

He then becomes responsible for her life, and his 007 has no problem cutting down any foes who block their paths. Dalton’s Bond was more polished than Connery and less comedic than Moore, but he was led by a kind of stormy passion. He had heart, but it pumped cold blood.

Still, Dalton had to break through that formidable wall of consensus. Bond is not a solo enterprise, of course. The director has a say, as do the producers and the screenwriters, but the danger comes from two places: the studio and the audience itself. As Dalton said, “Everybody in the world has got a different opinion about it,” and those voices have to be defied, just as his 007 refused the order to place a bullet in the enemy cellist’s heart.

Nothing makes hands wring and sweat pour in a studio conference room like something unexpected, and know-it-all supposed “fans” often resist change while paradoxically demanding something fresh and new. He had two outings as Bond, the second being 1989’s License to Kill, in which 007 goes even more rogue, defying Her Majesty’s secret service to embark on a mission of revenge. Then the character became tied up in rights lawsuits for several years, and Dalton chose not to return.

When Daniel Craig took over 007 from Pierce Brosnan in 2005, purists complained about his blue eyes and blond hair. When they finally saw him bring the character to life in 2006’s Casino Royale, those critics soon fell silent. Craig gave Bond a soldier’s mindset. He was a battlefield veteran whose new uniform happens to be a dashing suit. He was successful for so long because as he added his own spin, he capitalized on the best of what came before: He was as rough as Connery and as lethal as Dalton. The humor and playfulness that defined Moore and Brosnan were still present, but they served as a mask for the kind of wounded vulnerability that Lazenby had introduced.

Pattinson is now free to pick and choose whatever qualities he likes from this Brotherhood of Bond, but if he is going to take anyone’s advice, it should be Dalton’s: “Whoever does it has got to have a very clear sense of what they want to achieve.”

Fortunately, no one tells Villeneuve what to do. The filmmaker has spent two decades establishing himself not just as a distinctive artist but as a reliable box-office draw. Those two qualities give him a powerful weapon—clout—that can stave off the onslaught of opinions that will inevitably surround this casting choice.

Just this Saturday, I moderated a Q&A with Villeneuve at the Golden Globe Foundation before an audience of students, and one young filmmaker named Oscar Flores asked the Dune filmmaker, “When is the right time for you to listen to feedback, and when is the right time to listen to yourself?”

Villeneuve gave an answer that perfectly matches Dalton’s advice and increases my optimism about his approach to Bond: “Honestly, people say that I’m humble, but that’s bullshit,” he said, only partly joking. “If I’m asking for $200 million for a movie, what kind of humility is that?”

The suggestion he made to the students was to not take on projects that are outside their control. Start small, stay true, and build up your skill set behind the camera while establishing a reputation that gives you defining power over the movies you make. He warned them not to follow the “siren” song of a big-budget offer if it means ceding control. Instead, Villeneuve said, he found it effective to build up his status with smaller films, moving from 2010’s more intimate Incendies to 2013’s harrowing Prisoners and 2015’s even more sprawling Sicario before leveling up in scale to 2016’s Arrival and 2017’s Blade Runner 2049.

“The thing that I did when I was a young filmmaker is that I tried not to jump steps,” he told us at the Golden Globes Foundation forum. “I went step-by-step on projects. I was able to keep control of them. I was making sure that I was in control of the tools, and control of the language, and getting strong with the crew, being able to and making sure that my voice would be heard. … What I will say to you is: Be careful of the sirens.”

Only after amassing this authority, Villeneuve said, did he feel the time was right to take on his dream project of Dune. “If you made a successful first feature film that cost $200,000 and they offer you a major blockbuster, be careful,” he said this weekend. “Be careful who you are working with, and don’t listen to your ego. Do you really believe that you have the skills to bring it to the screen? Maybe you do. Some people can, by the way. I was not ready to make Dune after my second [film]. ... It took me a lot of movies before I knew that I was able to handle the pressure and making sure that I would be able to control totally, 100 percent, the film. At the end of the day when I look at the movie and the screen, it’s my movie.”

No doubt Pattinson’s casting was his decision, and Villeneuve’s status ensured it was accepted by the powers that be at Amazon MGM Studios. It probably wasn’t a difficult yes, given that Pattinson is currently in his prime. Not only is he The Batman, but he stood out as the conniving usurper Antinous in Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey and has earned accolades for his haunting performance as a predator-hunting journalist in Primetime.

Is he a heartthrob? Ask the legion of Twilight fans, and cover your ears for their deafening reply.

Pattinson forged his bond with Villeneuve with another upcoming role in Dune: Part Three, playing the Face Dancer Scytale. He’s a shape-shifting psychological assassin who seeks to undermine Timothée Chalamet’s all-seeing, all-powerful emperor by getting inside his head, and the actor and filmmaker worked closely to figure out the look, mannerisms, and motivations of a creature void of form.

As Pattinson said in a recent cover story I wrote for Empire magazine about Dune: Part Three: “I went into it being like, ‘I want to look like an alien,’ and initially Denis was saying, ‘Well, I think he’s got to fit in with everybody. I think he just looks like you. He’s normal,’ ” Pattinson said. The actor was candid about his own reaction: “I was a little bit disappointed.”

Then Villeneuve thought about it some more. As the director told those students on Saturday, he has a strong point of view, but it’s not inflexible. Villeneuve is not interested in the whims of a crowd, but he is very open to suggestions from creative partners. If their own insights open his eyes to a possibility he hadn’t considered, he will seize upon the ideas.

Shortly before shooting, Villeneuve reversed course on Scytale, and Pattinson said the filmmaker sent him an email with a new look for the Face Dancer. Pale skin, white hair, no eyebrows, which has the effect of making his expressions somewhat inscrutable. In other words, a blank page, upon which the characteristics of others can be written as he shifts his own natural shape. Pattinson said he felt relief: “I’m like, ‘Okay, this is very much not like a normal guy at all.’ ”

This anecdote is meaningful for 007 because it shows that the filmmaker and the star can share ideas, experiment and explore, and listen to each other. That’s the collaboration this character needs.

At 40, Pattinson is not the youngest possible Bond, but he brings life experience that will be vital for playing a character who needs a certain prowess and wisdom to survive. He and Villeneuve both have a kind of double-0 swagger that will allow them to ignore the cacophonous advice of the masses and devise something original.

Pattinson has been doing this for a long time. Consider his doomed Cedric Diggory in 2005’s Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire, another character—like Bond, Bruce Wayne, and Edward Cullen—who exists as an intense, preexisting image in the minds of millions of fans. But with his brooding presence, he added uncertainty to Cedric’s innate decency and fair-mindedness. The kid who dramatically hops down from a tree in his jump-scare introduction to Ron and Harry could very well have been there to bully them. That’s a sneak-attack entrance, proposed by Pattinson himself, and little details like that gave this upright, wholesome character a dose of intrigue.

Pattinson was only 17 when he filmed that. In the more than two decades that followed, he has paid his dues, learned his lessons, and no doubt gotten his share of scars. With Villeneuve backing him, he’s bulletproof. The two of them can take chances, be bold, and defy orders—just like 007 would, just as playing the character demands.

Pattinson is not a paper doll that will fold when the committees come for James Bond.

#6934. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6932. “Sexy, Weirdo Bond”: Why Women Love Robert Pattinson’s Casting as 007

It feels like we might finally get a more vulnerable, less sexist James Bond.

Whether you were waiting with bated breath or not, the years-long wait to find out who the new James Bond is over, with the announcement that everyone’s fave Gen Z Robert Pattinson is set to take the reins.

In many ways, his casting is totally unsurprising—he is, seemingly, Hollywood’s only leading man right now, appearing in The Drama, The Odyssey, and Primetime this year alone (with Dune: Part Three still to come), on top of being literal Batman. And yet, at the same time, Rob as Bond feels like a break with tradition. I mean, he’s still a handsome straight white guy — but he’s not your typical alpha male.

There’s a silliness, darkness, and freakiness (complimentary) to Pattinson and the characters he’s played. With projects famously traversing both indie flicks and major blockbusters, he tends to appear as more fuckboy, weirdo, or Bond villain than the emotionally distant, hunky womanizer typically synonymous with 007. And women in particular seem to be into it.

I’m not just talking about women who grew up crushing on Cedric Diggory and Edward Cullen, either. Not to assign too much weight to the James Bond franchise, but Pattinson’s casting comes at a time when we’re grappling with what masculinity means today. In an era when misogynistic manosphere figureheads are as prevalent on our screens as Hollywood stars, there’s less appetite for clichéd portrayals of the archetypal alpha Bond guy, who’s super jacked, can’t express his emotions, and treats women like disposable objects.

For heterosexual women, this extends to our romantic lives, too. A new survey by female-led dating app Chyrpe found that over half of women wouldn’t actively look for an “alpha male” in a partner, with 70 percent saying they’d rather date a supportive, emotionally available man who’s happy to let them take the lead (Bond as a sub?!).

“The alpha male is traditionally understood as a man who is dominant, assertive, and accustomed to being in control,” says Gigi Engle, relationship expert for Chyrpe. “There’s an assumption that he’s emotionally self-sufficient, sexually dominant, and rarely, if ever, vulnerable. Psychologically, this represents a pretty narrow and restrictive understanding of masculinity.”

Despite what the manosphere would have you believe, this performance of masculinity doesn’t work for anyone involved. “When men feel they have to constantly be this ‘dominant’ character, there’s often very little room for emotional openness or genuine partnership,” continues Engle. “Women may simply be getting tired of having to accommodate someone else’s need to feel powerful. It’s boring, frankly.” In fact, the same Chyrpe survey found that 33 percent of men feel pressured by social media to embody the alpha male stereotype, while 30 percent say male friends ridicule them if they don’t take on an alpha role in their relationship.

There’s an opportunity, then, for macho franchises like James Bond to change things. “There’s a growing thirst for a masculinity that feels more human and less traditional,” says Engle. “For decades, popular culture has sold us the idea that an attractive man is someone who is powerful, emotionally restrained, physically strong, and always in control. But those expectations are starting to feel dated and lame. We’re becoming more interested in men who can hold complexity. We want guys who are comfortable with women who don’t need them to take charge.”

It feels like in Pattinson’s hands, we might see 007’s softer, nerdier, more emotional side—which could be a good thing for both men and women.

At least, that’s what a bunch of women I spoke to are hoping for. “He’s a James Bond you can see shooting the bad guys and also snuggling up in front of the telly watching Bridget Jones—and he’s kinda awkward,” says Clare.

Another Claire tells me: “I just cannot imagine him enthusiastically doing some of the cringier, misogynistic lines or scenes with female costars. I think he’ll be able to bring charisma, equally he doesn’t try to overpower his female costars. He holds his own in a scene without it ever needing to take anything away from the people he’s sharing that scene with.”

Nicole says a “different portrayal of masculinity would be nice,” but if we don’t get that, “he should be bisexual at least.”

Others, including Engle, are excited by the darkness Pattinson might bring to Bond. “He has an unconventional screen presence, to say the least,” she says. “There’s an introspective, slightly unpredictable quality to him that feels different from the traditionally hyper-masculine Bond we’ve become accustomed to. I’d love to see a Bond who retains the confidence, danger, and sexual charisma of the character but is allowed to have real emotions.”

Another woman I spoke to, Kitty, feels similarly: “First of all, [Pattinson] is my all-time crush, but I’m excited because I wonder if they’ll take the opportunity to make him darker and explore the theme of him being an alcoholic and reclusive. Similar to how he made Batman emo, will they make Bond more moody? Because so many people have explored how fucked up [Bond] actually is as a person, it would be cool to bring it to the front.”

Or, as Elise sums it up: “Let’s fucking go for sexy, weirdo Bond.”

Until the first film drops in 2028, we’ll have no idea how Pattinson will play Bond—but there’s no harm in projecting our emotionally intelligent fantasies onto him in the meantime.

#6931. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6930. Sean Duffy Is a Perfect Reality-TV Politician. Too Bad for America.

As a fellow MTV veteran, I recognize that the transportation secretary has been practicing for this moment since his Real World days.

Yesterday, in the wake of Friday’s grounding of all flights in the continental United States and the weekend of cancellations and chaos that ensued, Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy made the rounds of the Sunday-morning political talk shows to make this face.

It’s been a tough few days for Duffy, and Duffy has been huffy. From Friday’s press conference with Markwayne Mullin, to his own microphonically challenged solo outing on Saturday, through all five morning-show appearances, Duffy has checked all the boxes the modern Republican official must: look aggrieved, place blame elsewhere, praise the president, be white. It’s all television, after all, and there’s a role to be played.

Sean Duffy has been at this for decades. You know he was on the sixth season of The Real World, and you know he met his wife and fellow Real Worlder Rachel Campos when they participated in an early season of what would become The Real World/Road Rules Challenge. But when you really go back and look, you’ll see that they’ve both been practicing for this moment since the ’90s. Game recognizes game; as a fellow alumnus of 20th-century MTV, let me walk you through it.

The Real World was born when MTV decided to try its hand at creating a weekly soap opera, in the style of the era-defining Beverly Hills 90210 and Melrose Place. But cable budgets being what they are, a full scripted show was untenable, so producers had the bold idea to do away with actors and writers entirely. The first couple seasons of The Real World kept the focus on art by casting young artists: Julie the emerging dancer amid the model and writer and singer-songwriters in season 1, Jon the wannabe Garth Brooks in season 2. But that went out the window quick, as producers and aspiring castmates figured out the same thing at the same time: The job was to stir the pot. So by season 3, deeply unpleasant bike messenger Puck was dropped into the San Francisco house to clash with saintly AIDS educator Pedro, with whom conservative Rachel initially refused to share a bathroom for fear of sink-based HIV transmission. In 1994.

By season 6, casts seemed chosen specifically to play a predetermined part and drive each other up the wall. Real World: Boston features Stanford med student Kameelah and ladies man Syrus, Catholic Texan Elka and Manhattan-born mess on wheels Montana. And then there was Sean Duffy, Wisconsin boy, lumberjack-games participant, and future secretary of transportation. Sean occupied the Cute Boy spot in the cast that had been held by Aaron in Los Angeles and St. Louis race car driver Mike in London. But this being 1997, he was also a different cultural archetype: the knee-jerk Rush Limbaugh–era conservative. Accent on jerk. Here’s a moment when Sean makes fun of Elka’s underwear and then immediately denies it.

Just straight-up, reflexively, smoothly denying he did something we just saw him do, because he did it on camera and into a microphone. Lying with a straight face. This would become a job skill.

It’s a little like something we saw Sean’s future wife Rachel do on her season. In an early episode, she engages in the hot, safe rebellious act of 1994 by getting her navel pierced. In a later episode, she gets a telephone call from her angry mother, who has seen the belly-button bauble in a photograph (a physical photograph, sent through the U.S. Mail—again, we lived differently in the ’90s). Rachel assures her mother it’s not a piercing but instead a sticker she put there. Forget the fact that, again, all of this is on camera and the mom is going to learn the truth soon enough. Watching back then, I remember thinking two things: One, this person lies very easily, and two, I think she might be one of those people who’s conservative just so they don’t upset their parents.

Anyway. Sean and Rachel did The Challenge and got married and had three hundred and fifty children. Sean became a congressman for Wisconsin; Rachel tried out for The View but never quite nailed it and now she’s on Fox & Friends sometimes. And because it all comes back to reality television and personal branding, the Duffy family spent a few weeks of Sean’s tenure as secretary of transportation to film a show. The Great American Road Trip was supposed to have aired on Fox Nation, but instead it got dumped onto YouTube, where most episodes are hovering around 10k views.

Also, Duffy’s son-in-law Michael Alfonso, who’s 26, is set to become the youngest person in Congress, having won a seat in Wisconsin’s 7th District in this month’s elections. Alfonso’s campaign was buoyed by generous campaign donations from numerous transportation companies, executives, and lobbyists, including a $2,500 contribution from Air Space Intelligence’s PAC in March. In June, the FAA awarded the AI start-up a contract worth up to $875.95 million. The company’s SMART system went nationwide days before the November 13 ground stop, though the FAA and the company said the outage was unrelated to SMART.

I’m sure it’s nothing. But I’m even more sure Duffy will say it is.

The way that John F. Kennedy understood television in the way Richard Nixon did not, Sean Duffy understands reality television in a way few others do. He’s lived it. He understands it as a marketing exercise. Knowing your brand, hitting the beats of the scene you’re supposed to play, showing up with a yearbook picture-day haircut and lying with a smile will get you far in this world. Whether he survives this turbulence or not, he’s learned that lesson.

Sean and Rachel have been at this for decades, but as viewers so have we. We can see them as they are: dishonest children, playing a role, begging for approval, just trying not to get yelled at.

#6929. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research from Digital Realty.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

#6927. Is There a Right Way to Respond to a Grammy Snub?

Artists like John Summit and MUNA have reacted to being shut out by the Recording Academy. But is there a correct way to approach it?

Not to go all “question for the culture” on everyone, but this year’s Grammy nominations have been on my mind for too long for me not to ask: Is there really a “right” way to acknowledge being snubbed by the Academy?

While the Recording Academy granted cultural aficionados the pleasant surprise of recognizing longtime underdogs like Raye, Remi Wolf, Tinashe, and Underscores, some names were noticeably absent from this year’s nominations list—and they had something to say about it.

For starters, EDM giant John Summit made headlines for his unfiltered reaction to being left out of the Dance and Electronic categories.

“never submitting to the grammys again unless shit changes, it’s all a fucking joke,” he wrote, criticizing the Academy for only recognizing “music industry fueled pop stars.”

After receiving backlash from online bystanders—including Charli xcx, who he previously requested to hop on a track in 2024—he walked back his claims, writing, “i realized i didn’t do a good job of making my point earlier. my intent was not to drag other artists down and i seriously congratulate everyone who was nominated. they fully deserve the recognition. i just think there should be an extra category for more dance music djs & producers.”

John isn’t wrong to point out the Academy’s standards in certain categories, but his delivery was a bit aggressive. Especially after he seemingly diminished fans’ opinions and deemed them “lil teeny bopper pop stan accounts with zero real world life experience.” It’s…a choice to say that about the people who move culture forward.

Naturally, his reaction got me thinking about the unspoken etiquette of addressing a snub. It’s valid to be upset over not being recognized by your peers, as well as seasoned vets in the biz. But you don’t want to ruin your chances for redemption, either.

Take Noah Kahan, for example. He reassured his fans that he’s “done everything I’ve ever needed to do in my career because of YOUR support” ahead of the nominations, only to be shut out in the core categories: Record of the Year, Song of the Year, and Album of the Year. Though his album The Great Divide still earned a nod in the Best Contemporary Folk Album category, he acknowledged his snub with three words: “Life goes on.” Simple, yet effective!

Another artist who didn’t land a nod was pop princess and Cosmo cover girl Kim Petras, who made history as the first openly transgender woman to win a Grammy back in 2023. Her career took a tumultuous turn when she signed a major-label deal after riding the success of her award-winning track with Sam Smith, “Unholy.”

Years into her deal, she publicly accused her label of gatekeeping funds and resources, and pleaded to be released from her contract. She ultimately returned to her independent roots for her latest record, Detour, funding most of the studio sessions, visuals, and live performances herself. When the album didn’t earn a nod, fans claimed she was “snubbed,” since her hard work deserved recognition.

“I wouldn’t say so … I just left the industry tbh and I made the music to make the music cause I love it so much,” she wrote in response, claiming that her “nomination days [are] over” as she roots for Tinashe and Underscores.

The self-proclaimed “Greatest Band in the World,” MUNA, also started gaining momentum since trading a larger label for Phoebe Bridgers’ independent Saddest Factory Records in 2022. Their latest album, Dancing on the Wall, drew massive crowds at festivals like Lollapalooza and All Things Go last summer. The Academy has yet to acknowledge them, despite continued success.

The band’s lead singer, Katie Gavin, released a short video addressing fans after nominations dropped, saying, “I wasn’t expecting it, and I understand it. And it’s tough for a band like us because we make pop music, but we’re not as big as other pop artists.”

She added that they attempted to submit for alternative categories, but they ultimately didn’t make the cut because of their inherent pop sound. In the end, she kept things candid.

To put it plainly, there isn’t a definitive right or wrong way to go about being slept on. If artists decide to publicly address their snubs in the future, they should grab their pens and papers and take notes from Katie’s final sentiment.

“I’m really proud of all the people that did get nominated. There was so much amazing music this year,” she shared. “I hate being disappointed, but I’m not giving up hope.”

#6926. Our 10 Favorite Men’s Fisherman Sweaters to Wear All Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The coziest, most rugged layers a man could ever buy.

We love fisherman sweaters for the same reason we love a great martini: They’re classic, and they’re comforting. A proper fisherman knit brings the whole winter wardrobe into alignment. The chunky texture instantly upgrades jeans and boots, warmth doesn’t sacrifice style, and that easy, rugged-prep vibe that works at the office as well as it does on the couch. In other words, it’s the rare cold-weather staple that’s as practical as it is handsome.

Now, it’s crucial to remember that when it comes to the backstory of most garments, there tends to be a lot of lore mixed in with the genuine history. That said, here’s how the story generally goes. Traditional fisherman sweaters hail from Ireland, where in the late 1800s to the mid-1900s, they were popular for men who had to work long hours on fishing boats. Usually, fisherman sweaters were hand-knit by their wives or by local artisans, and they were always designed to be sturdy and warm, keeping the wind and chill out. Cable-knit patterns became popular for fisherman sweaters. You might know these sweaters as Aran sweaters, referencing the Aran Islands, where the textured design has become a specialty.

In 2026, you probably don’t need a fisherman sweater to keep you warm while you’re on the water off Ireland’s west coast. (Or maybe you do! We don’t know your story.) However, you do need a fisherman sweater for the sheer coziness and looks they provide, though. Our favorite options of all time are below, tested and reviewed by Esquire editors (with some photographed on Esquire’s editor in chief, Michael Sebastian). Anyway, these fisherman sweaters are seriously making us want to take some fishing lessons. Anyone know where we can do that?

You don’t need to break the bank for a high-quality fisherman sweater that’ll last you years to come. Quince is proof. Crafted from organic cotton, this thick, warm sweater is a perfect layering piece for winter—put a tee or a button-down underneath, a topcoat or a puffer on top, and you’re golden. The rib detailing offers some extra texture and style, and relaxed raglan sleeves offer more freedom of movement.

Ok, so the pictured sweater is no longer in stock. The cable knit rollneck was seasonal, but there is always a fisherman-y option in the collection. Honestly, whichever J. Crew rollneck hits this bar at any point in time, that’s likely my favorite fisherman sweater of the moment.

L.L. Bean crafts a classic fisherman sweater to a tee. It has everything you’d want in the style: a thick, heavyweight fabric that carries some structure with it; a cable-knit design; and thick cotton yarn that keeps the chill out and the warmth in. This style is a go-to for something timeless without too much fuss or frill. It has a slim fit, so if you want something baggier, more classically cut, make sure to size up.

Okay, hear me out—this isn’t your traditional fisherman sweater, but you can make a damn good excuse for Polo Ralph Lauren’s ultra-soft, ultra-luxe cable-knit sweater when you feel how unbelievably plush this is. This is the fisherman sweater you go to when you want something distinctly elevated and chic but you still want to have a cable-knit design and a fit you can dress down with joggers and slides.

You may already know that we at Esquire have an affinity for shawl collar cardigans. With their laid-back Western vibes and classic Americana silhouette, shawl collar cardigans look handsome on just about any guy. One of our favorites is this fisherman style from Filson. It’s made from thick, warm cotton and features a classic button front and ribbing on the hem and cuffs.

If you, like commerce editor Luke Guillory, aren’t into the super chunky fisherman sweaters and are looking for something a little more lightweight that you can easily layer, this Buck Mason pick should be right up your alley. Luke has even endorsed the style, lauding the sweater for its warm yet lightweight feel, perfect not-too-boxy but not-too-slim cut, and durable cotton that can be machine washed and still not miss a beat.

This gorgeous wool fisherman sweater is from Finisterre, a surf clothing brand from Cornwall, England, so it’s specifically made for cold, windy days on the water. It’s made from 100 percent heavyweight, pure new wool with a natural crimp, which means it creates natural pockets of air to help insulate you (even if you’re not on the water). One writer says it’s one of his go-to winter sweaters.

Brooks Brothers’ woolly sweater is so damn good I might just have to take up the art of fishing to pay proper tribute to it. With a soft and cozy yet breathable wool fabric and cable-knit pattern, this relaxed style puts a contemporary spin on the traditional fisherman sweater. It’s warm, soft, and stylish, and it looks just as good with dress pants and loafers as it does with jeans and sneakers.

Begg x Co’s Isla cashmere sweater is another fantastic, high-quality cashmere option. It’s just oppressively beautiful. Begg x Co operates its own mills in Scotland, which shows up in the world-class materials and construction, but the vibe isn’t overly capital-F-Fashion the way some French or Italian luxury brands can feel. It’s timeless, understated luxury. Made from 8‑ply cashmere, it lands in a perfect mid-weight sweet spot for fall and winter: substantial and warm without being bulky. It’s definitely quite pricey, but you’re paying for the highest level of quality and finish.

This sweater from Ghiaia Cashmere is divine, surprising considering the brand is based out of southern California. The mock neck is the star here—cozy without the bulk of a turtleneck—and the rich brown color is lovely (though it also comes in a clean cream if you want something lighter). A chest pocket adds a surprisingly practical touch, making it feel more functional than you’d ever expect a sweater to be.

#6923. Why Do Fans Hate to See an Up-and-Coming Male Actor With a “Normie” GF? Unpacking This Parasocial Phenomenon

From Belmont Cameli to Christopher Briney, fans are fully losing the plot.

Amid breakup rumors (which were almost certainly manufactured), Belmont Cameli stepped out with his TV writer girlfriend Raina Morris for a West Hollywood date night on November 10. The two were together before Off Campus premiered, catapulting Belmont into the public eye, but his fans remain convinced that (1) Belmont is meant to be with his costar Ella Bright and (2) Raina must have an ulterior motive in dating him—that motive, of course, being tangential fame.

Here are a few select comments from Just Jared’s post about the outing on Instagram, to give you an idea of what I’m talking about:

As a reminder: This all comes a couple of months after Raina was forced to release a statement asking Off Campus fans to stop harassing her family. “If you are a fan of my boyfriend: Yay!” she started her message, adding, “Please stop messaging my family members. They are so confused. Write in your diary and then go on a walk instead! Love u.”

Raina is not the only non-actor girlfriend of a rising star to experience this kind of cruel harassment. Similarly, when it came to light that Heated Rivalry star Hudson Williams was not dating his costar Connor Storrie in real life (as much as fans wanted that to be true) but was instead dating tattoo artist Katelyn Rose Larson (and had been for years), some people felt a certain type of way.

Hudson first introduced fans to his girlfriend with an Instagram Story post on Valentine’s Day 2026 (two months after Deuxmoi revealed her identity to the public), noting that Katelyn has been with him “since my 2000 gold Mazda Protégé smoked and squealed and I had no job.” But still, when Katelyn joined Hudson on the red carpet at an Oscars after-party the next month, some fans weren’t shy about making their disapproval known...and they’ve been keeping it up ever since.

Again, I present a few comments from a Deuxmoi post showing the two hugging before Heated Rivalry season 2 filming kicked off back in August:

A month after those paparazzi shots of their hug went viral, Katelyn took to her Instagram Stories to write, “Obviously a lot of you have found the studio because of a certain show and that’s more than okay. This is where I draw a very hard line: booking an appointment with me or anyone else purely because of the connection to the show crosses a huge territory.”

She added, “Showing up to your appointment and asking my coworkers any sort of questions about myself or about Hudson is hugely uncomfortable for everyone.”

And of course, we can’t have this conversation without mentioning Christopher Briney’s girlfriend, fellow actor Isabel Machado, who he started dating in college and who has faced no shortage of harassment from The Summer I Turned Pretty fans...much of it due to the fact that she kiiiiinda looks like his onscreen love interest, played by Lola Tung.

I present to you: select comments from a Deuxmoi post announcing Chris and Isabel’s engagement (though that rumor turned out to be totally false).

And while Isabel has never released a statement directly calling out fans, she seemed to respond to harassment in her own subtle way with an August 2025 Instagram post reminding everyone that she’s a “person with feelings.”

So...why is it that fans hate to see an up-and-coming male actor with a less-famous girlfriend? For starters, I don’t think it’s a coincidence that all three of these men star in beloved TV romances...or that their fandoms all want them to be with their onscreen love interests IRL.

But Ella Bright and Belmont Cameli are not actually Hannah Wells and Garrett Graham. And while the actors seem to have great chemistry both onscreen and offscreen, fans must remember that they’re actors for a reason: They’re good at fooling audiences. And dating someone other than their costar doesn’t mean they’re fooling themselves.

These three rising stars have also all been in relationships since before their breakout roles, so to accuse their partners of social climbing seems not only rude, but straight-up false. While they may not have millions of followers, these supposed “normie” girlfriends are all successful in their own right, and it’s been made clear that none of them are trying to steal the spotlight.

“She’s not trying to be out there or be perceived,” Hudson said of Katelyn to ETalk Canada back in March. “She’s always like, ‘I don’t want to be perceived!’”

While Belmont doesn’t talk about Raina directly, he did address the “real venom” of “fandom these days,” telling The Hollywood Reporter, “You can say whatever you want about me or people in my life, but it’s not going to affect me because I’m fully aware of what my reality looks like and feels like.”

Chris also expressed a similar sentiment to Elite Daily when talking about his relationship with Isabel, saying, “People have definitely crossed lines and stepped on personal boundaries...I’ve learned to separate myself from social media and understand that I have my own life. If people have opinions about it, all right, that’s fine.”

Still, it seems fans cannot stop hurling accusations and spreading breakup rumors whenever they’re reminded that Conrad Fisher is not their boyfriend...or Belly Conklin’s boyfriend...or even a real person. Yes, these so-called nepo girlfriends have gained more followers than they may have if their boyfriends were still struggling actors. But chances are, they would have stuck with their men even if they never landed their breakout roles (and are still with them despite what may be totally unwanted attention).

Point being: You can ‘ship your fave fictional relationship all you want. You can even think, “Why not me?” when you see your fave TV heartthrob with someone else. But when you come for the very real people in their lives, you’ve done more than lose the plot; you’ve crossed into parasocial behavior.

To quote Hudson: “RPFing gotta stop, y’all.”

#6922. I Have Serious Questions About the 2026 Grammy Nominations

Is the Recording Academy out of its mind?

The Grammys, more than any other awards-voting body of the big EGOT four, is easily the most chaotic. Sure, we can shake our fists in the air all day about how Stanley Kubrick somehow never won an Oscar, or ask ourselves why the Tony Awards ever allowed themselves to become a place where SpongeBob SquarePants: The Broadway Musical leads with 12 nominees. But recalling Grammy events like Macklemore beating Kendrick Lamar, Esperanza Spalding claiming Best New Artist over Justin Bieber and Drake, and Beck taking home Album of the Year instead of Beyoncé, it’s a wonder just what happens between when the votes are submitted and when the winners are revealed.

This year? The Recording Academy announced on Monday, November 16, that Kanye West is a Grammy nominee once again. And there’s a single category where Bruce Springsteen, Phoebe Bridgers, and Charli XCX are lumped together, so I guess they’re considered basically the same kind of artist to voters. Plus, new ideas like “Best Asian Pop Music Performance” are moving forward despite immediately losing support from the most prominent artist in that category, and Best New Artist nominees can apparently put out their fifth studio album and still be considered. What’s going on?

So, before the Grammys decide to ignore all of our desires and complaints and just shower Taylor Swift with more little golden phonographs on February 7, 2027, let’s try to make sense of the 2026 Grammy nominees below.

The first thought that entered my mind when I saw this year’s nominees for Album of the Year was, Are we confirming who will be available to perform before announcing the nominees now? Because even if so, I think Bruno Mars would perform either way.

It’s very cool to see how women dominated the music field this year, especially because they truly did. Olivia Rodrigo, Phoebe Bridgers, and Rosalía all earning Album of the Year nominations is a no-brainer. Olivia Dean, who won Best New Artist last year, and Ella Langley, who wrote “Choosin’ Texas,” make up the rest of the list. (Did we really love that entire nineteen-track Langley album?) Plus, of course, the obligatory Taylor Swift nominations.

But were Bruno Mars and Leon Thomas—who are tied with seven nominations each after the leading women, including an Album of the Year nod for Mars—the two men with the best offerings this year?

Bruno Mars had a very good song on The Romantic with “Why You Wanna Fight?” The Luther Vandross-esque R&B ballad was everything you’d look for in a Song of the Year nominee. But instead, it looks like Bruno is going all in on “Risk It All” and “I Just Might,” the latter of which is more of a “Happy” by Pharrell Williams-type song that somehow isn’t the lead single of the next Trolls movie.

Sorry Madonna! Apologies Robyn, Tinashe, Slayyter, and every other pop artist who soundtracked our year on the dance floor with some of the best albums of 2026. Breaking into the top four major categories still looks like a no-go.

Instead, Madonna, Robyn, and Tinashe were relegated to categories such as Best Dance Pop Recording and Best Dance/Electronic Album, even despite critical acclaim. If you want a Record of the Year nod, try writing a song for Toy Story 5.

Don Toliver is up for Best New Artist. The Houston rapper, who was signed to Travis Scott’s label in 2018, released his fifth studio album, Octane, this year. He had a song on the F9 soundtrack in 2021—which isn’t even the latest Fast & Furious movie—and his 2023 album, Love Sick, featured James Blake, Future, and Justin Bieber. But here we are, in 2026, watching him compete for Best New Artist.

Equally puzzling: Remi Wolf is nominated for Best New Artist. The singer’s exceptional debut record, Juno, came out in 2021. And her best record to date, Mud, was released recently on October 9, just outside of the Grammy submission window that closed in late August. But following the logic of how Olivia Dean won Best New Artist last year even though Dean’s album is competing this year, it’s clear that the whole Best New Artist category needs some rethinking.

2026 wasn’t a banner year for rap. I can admit that. Sure, we had very good records from A$AP Rocky, Denzel Curry, Vince Staples, JPEGMAFIA, Isaiah Rashad, Genesis Owusu, and Baby Keem. But none of the musicians I listed above went recognized this year, except for Keem’s single “Best Rap Performance” nomination.

Instead, the Recording Academy nominated Wale’s Everything Is a Lot—the Washington, D.C., rapper’s best and most mature album of the last decade, but far less influential than Rocky’s or Keem’s releases. And if not for Drake’s long-running boycott, the absence of Iceman’s “Janice STFU” just looks silly. But if you want to talk ridiculous, let’s see how Rap Album of the Year nominee Kanye West plays out for the Recording Academy over the next couple of months.

Harry Styles won Album of the Year for Harry’s House in 2023. He beat out Un Verano Sin Ti by Bad Bunny, Renaissance by Beyoncé, Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers by Kendrick Lamar, and 30 by Adele. It was a major upset. And just last month, the former One Direction member wrapped a historic thirty-night residency at Madison Square Garden in New York City in support of his latest album, Kiss All the Time. Disco, Occasionally.

Now, I’m not saying Kiss All the Time is a masterwork, but it’s strange that Styles didn’t receive a single nomination for his Harry’s House follow-up. “Aperture” was very catchy! Taylor Swift can’t sneeze without the Recording Academy inventing a new category for her to compete in. But an artist who just won Album of the Year can find himself completely shut out, even if his latest record is a success?

The global K-pop megastars reunited this year following mandatory military service in South Korea for one of the genre’s most-anticipated albums of all time, ARIRANG. The record paid homage to the popular Korean folk song of the same name while also pushing the K-pop genre forward with hip-hop-heavy material. ARIRANG’s fourteen tracks also filled the top fourteen spots of Spotify’s global Top 50 chart on its first day of release on March 20. I’ll stop there, but there are a hundred more ways to say that it was one of the biggest albums of the year.

But when the Recording Academy announced a new category this year, “Best Asian Pop Music Performance,” the group made the decision not to submit for the Grammys this year. The move was widely regarded as a protest of the new category, which many interpreted as a ghettoization to keep the rise of popular Asian acts away from the general field categories. “I hope that music can be heard and loved for what it is, rather than being divided by region or language,” the group posted on social media.

On Monday, November 16, the Academy went through with the category anyway, announcing nominees including Lisa from Blackpink, the Japanese pop-metal group Babymetal, the K-pop group IVE, and the Filipino girl group Bini, among others. But with BTS joining Drake in his boycott of the Grammys, I wonder how the Recording Academy will fare without two of the world’s most popular acts entertaining its awards show.

The Great Divide, a seventeen-track rock album that turned Kahan into one of the most popular touring musicians of 2026, was considered a favorite to receive a nomination in every major category this year. Songs like “American Cars” dealt with past failures and road trip blues, while lead single “The Great Divide” tackled how fame added extra struggles to many of his already-strained relationships. And Kahan had the resume to become a major category contender this year. He had a Number One record! He did the full press tour, he performed on Saturday Night Live, and he sold out stadiums all across the country.

But it must have been an omen when Kahan sang that “California’s so much more than some awards show” on the deluxe edition’s “Orbiter,” because he received just one nomination on Monday for Best Contemporary Folk Album. It’s a shame.

Best Album Cover is back. The category returned last year and accurately awarded Tyler, the Creator’s zany Chromakopia artwork, which featured the artist wearing a black, ceramic mask. He told Complex at the time that it symbolized how the new music was “so honest, that I think I had to wear a mask of my own face to get some of that sh*t out.”

But what about this year’s nominees? Well, Mavis Staples’s Sad and Beautiful World artwork depicts the artist sitting at a table. Interpol’s This Mirror Weighs a Ton artwork is a chandelier made of security cameras, and Lily Allen’s West End Girl is a painting of the artist wearing a polka-dot jacket. Are these nominees... the best album covers of the year?

Charli XCX’s photograph of Martin Scorsese, John Cale, and Marc Jacobs was easily the much-talked-about album cover of the year, even though Music, Fashion, Film wasn’t nominated. Olivia Rodrigo’s upside-down swinging for you seem pretty sad for a girl so in love is definitely one of the more memorable covers that come to mind. Same goes for Robyn’s blurry and nude Sexistential photo, the politics behind Kneecap’s Fenian, Thundercat biting his dark leather jacket on the cover of Distracted, or even a pregnant Latto posing with a baby tiger for the Big Mama cover ahead of her cheetah-themed baby shower.

We might not have a specific shade of green to color this year and make Best Album Cover an easy choice, but if you’re going to bring the category back? At least wow us.

#6921. Jackie Kennedy’s Private Letters to Truman Capote Reveal a Fuller Picture of Their Friendship

Heading to auction on November 21, the six notes are estimated to fetch as much as $80,000.

First Lady Jacqueline Kennedy’s sister, Lee Radziwill, was one of celebrated author Truman Capote’s “Swans,” developing a close, if complicated, friendship with the novelist. But Jackie had her own relationship with Capote, one that is less widely known. The pair corresponded with each other in the 1960s and Kennedy may have even gotten an early look at Capote’s 1966 novel, In Cold Blood.

“I have your precious book and am so overjoyed,” she wrote to Capote in an undated card, presumed to be from June 1963. “The minute Jack takes off for Europe I will retreat to the country + indulge myself by reading every page—not just the new ones.”

That letter, along with five others between Kennedy and Capote, will soon hit the auction block, ELLE Decor can reveal. The notes, three of which are written on White House letterhead, will be sold together in a single lot through Cornell Auctions, estimated to fetch between $40,000 and $80,000. Together, they reveal new details about the friendship between the former first lady and one of America’s great authors.

Available for the first time ever, these private letters are part of a larger auction of Capote-linked items accumulated over decades by his biographer, Gerald Clarke, author of 1988’s Capote. The sale, “The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote,” will take place on November 21.

Kennedy’s notes are all believed to have been written in 1963 and 1964, with four bearing postmark dates. During that period, Kennedy suffered personal tragedies, losing her infant son Patrick days after he was born as well as her husband, President John F. Kennedy, who was assassinated in November 1963.

The letters indicate a warm relationship between Kennedy and Capote. In several of the messages, the first lady makes reference to various presents and mementos she received from the writer.

One particularly meaningful gift came in the wake of President John F. Kennedy’s death, after Jackie and Capote saw each other at a gathering hosted by decorator Billy Baldwin. “The most beautiful porcelain rose that I ever saw or dreamed of—came to me the other day from you,” the former first lady wrote in a three-page December 1964 missive, handwritten on mourning stationery bearing a crest.

In another letter, Kennedy makes reference to a gift she sent to Capote, inspired by a visit to the novelist’s home. “In your Japanese-Victorian room I spotted two large apothecary jars of the same kind—I found this one in 1955 in Paris—so it has been with me for many years in many places,” she wrote in an undated letter, believed to be from December 1964.

Handwritten on black-bordered stationery, that note was addressed to Capote’s house at 70 Willow Street in Brooklyn Heights, where he wrote Breakfast at Tiffany’s and In Cold Blood.

Read Jackie Kennedy’s Letters to Truman Capote

Beyond the Kennedy notes, also up for auction is Capote’s four-piece formal dinner invitation from President Lyndon B. Johnson in 1966, which has an estimate of $600 to $2,000. Other highlights include a German pencil sharpener in a leather pouch; a chalkware bulldog; a monogrammed duffel bag; and jewel-like paperweights made by the author himself.

Taken together, these pieces paint a fuller picture of Capote’s life and work as a writer, as well as Kennedy’s enthusiastic support of his talents. “I keep thinking what power a great writer has. All the things you write move people,” she wrote. “I am glad you became a writer.”

“The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote” will take place November 21 starting at 11 a.m.; bidding can be done online via LiveAuctioneers or in person at Cornell Auctions in Bellport, New York.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.
