#6919. THE Journal Reveals 2026 Product of the Year Winners

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

THE Journal has announced the winners of its 2026 Product of the Year Awards. Since 2020, these awards have recognized outstanding product development achievements of manufacturers and suppliers whose products or services are considered to be particularly noteworthy in the transformation of education technology.

This year, 16 companies were recognized across multiple categories for their impact in education. For the full list of winners, visit THEJournal.com.

"Congratulations to all of our 2026 Product of the Year Award winners," said Rhea Kelly, editor-in-chief of THE Journal. "These innovative solutions reflect the continued evolution of education technology and the important role it plays in supporting educators, improving student experiences and helping institutions navigate an increasingly digital future. We're proud to recognize the companies driving meaningful progress across education institutions."

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

#6918. These Holiday Perfumes Smell Like the Most Wonderful Time of the Year

‘Tis the season... to smell really freaking good.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Call me Buddy the Elf, but IMO, there’s no such thing as getting too in the holiday spirit. I’ve been known to display multiple Christmas trees, I love putting together holiday cards, and cooking Christmas dinner for friends and family is one of my great joys in life. And of course, all of this is happening while I’m blasting Mariah Carey and all my other favorite holiday songs.

But don’t think I’ve forgotten about the beauty side of things! As a faithful beauty editor, I also get excited to change up my fragrance this time of year and embrace some holiday scents. Do I want to smell just like a Christmas tree? No, not really. But I do want to spritz something that’ll help get me in the holiday spirit. Think about all the best scents of the season—gingerbread, pine, cinnamon spice, vanilla... These make for perfect holiday perfumes. So, in the name of very important holiday beauty research, I’ve rounded up the best festive fragrances worth adding to your rotation this season. From cozy gourmand perfumes to fresh woodsy notes and everything in between, one spritz of any of these will instantly get you in the holiday spirit.

Nest’s famous holiday candle is a staple in my home, and now, I can wear the scent on my skin too. It bottles up that festive feeling with fir balsam, winter cranberry, and grapefruit for a perfume that’s equal parts fresh, fruity, and woodsy. Think wandering through a Christmas tree farm with a little tart cranberry cocktail. It’s festive without smelling like you’re hiding pine branches up your sleeves.

Holiday parties call for a little spiked eggnog, and Dior Spice Blend brings that same boozy, spicy energy to your fragrance wardrobe. Rich rum notes, fiery black pepper, and zesty ginger blend together for a warm, intoxicating, and spirited scent. I love that it’s bold, sophisticated, and much less sugary than a typical holiday gourmand.

You don’t actually have to know how to ski to appreciate a good après ski moment (bring on the hot chocolate and jacuzzi, please). Ellis Brooklyn’s wintry scent captures that exact winter getaway feeling, thanks to seasonal notes of juniper berries, cardamom, soft suede, and musk. It’s woodsy, a little romantic, and pretty much begging to be worn at a chic mountain lodge.

Hot chocolate is a holiday must-have for me, but let’s be real: The best part is the mountain of whipped cream on top. Philosophy Fresh Cream bottles up that sweet, fluffy deliciousness with notes of whipped cream, tonka bean, heliotrope, and sugar. It’s soft, milky, and comforting without any heaviness or spice. And at under $60, it’s a pretty sweet addition to your holiday fragrance wardrobe.

Dreaming of a white Christmas? Future Society Snow Beginnings can make that happen. It smells like the magic of a fresh snowfall, but with a surprisingly fruity, floral twist. Cool Japanese mint and sparkling pink grapefruit deliver that feeling of crisp winter air, while lychee, crystallized rose, and sake add a sweet warmth. It’s a winter wonderland in a bottle, no freezing temperatures or shoveling required.

Who says you have to spend a fortune to smell festive? Dove’s Holiday Treats Frosted Plum Berry Body Mist delivers a juicy, fruity take on holiday fragrance for just about the same price as your seasonal latte. The sweet, bright, and surprising plum feels playful and is a refreshing departure from the usual dessert-heavy holiday scents. Plus, it’s easy to layer with your favorite body lotion, scrub, or perfume for a little extra festive flair.

#6915. How to Talk to Your Friends and Family About the Trans Community, According to Experts

If you get flustered whenever you speak up about trans rights, you're not alone. That's why we put together this list of expert-approved talking points.

Since Donald Trump took his second term in office, he and his administration have issued a series of discriminatory executive orders restricting access to gender-affirming care for trans youth, banning trans women and girls from participating in women’s sports, declaring that the federal government will only recognize two genders (impacting identifying documents like passports and visas), banning transgender people from serving in the military, and so much more.

Thankfully, many of these executive orders are being challenged in court. But the messaging has already infiltrated the brains of Trump supporters (and beyond) far and wide, normalizing these hateful thoughts and actions in our country.

So what do we do? We speak out and spread facts. Especially with those closest to us who might not be informed or might not understand. That’s not always easy though, especially when emotions get involved and you end up getting upset and flustered. To help, I tapped three experts to create a list of responses to common (and problematic) refrains about the trans community you might come across in future discussions.

In any discourse with someone who doesn’t think like you, it’s always important to make sure the other party knows you’re actually also listening—not just arguing. But if tensions do get high, the Q&As below can serve as a script of sorts to fall back on when you want to make sure you’re hitting all the key talking points while supporting and speaking up for the trans community.

Dr. Meredith McNamara: Actually, some people are born with neither or with genitalia that doesn’t fit binary concepts of sex. What sex is someone who is born without a vagina but has XX chromosomes (MRKH Syndrome)? What sex is someone who has XXY chromosomes and little to no detectable testosterone levels in adulthood (Klinefelter syndrome)?

But let’s take a step back and look at this from a different angle: Gender and sex are different. Gender identity develops long before the physical change of puberty. People generally know their gender long before secondary sex characteristics develop, because gender identity has its own strong biological basis rooted in the brain and it exists separately from chromosomes, gonads, and hormones.

Gillian Branstetter: And why not? Every culture in human history has had people who reject their gender assignment in one way or another, and decades of feminist legal progress has challenged the idea that biology must be our destiny. Transgender people come from all walks of life—every ethnicity, every background, every country, every political party. All people deserve the freedom to be themselves without fear, and that includes transgender people.

Shane Diamond: Everybody has a gender: an internal sense of self as it relates to the gendered world around us. For many people, that inner sense of self matches the sex they were assigned at birth—for example, someone assigned female at birth who knows herself to be a woman. This simply means they are cisgender! For other people, their inner sense of self is different from their sex assigned at birth, causing an incongruence with who they know themselves to be and how the world sees them. This person might call themselves transgender. That doesn’t make anyone’s gender more or less valid than someone else’s—it just means they’re different. At the end of the day, we don’t expect everyone to know exactly what it means to be transgender, but we do expect people to respect each other, even when we are different.

Gillian Branstetter: Transgender people face discrimination, mistreatment, and abuse across our lives. We’re four times as likely to experience violence and much more likely to experience poverty and homelessness. While nobody should have to pay such a price just for being true to themselves, I hardly think anyone wants that kind of “attention.” Also, transgender people are raised around cisgender people all the time—it never seems to “rub off” on us.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: In my practice, I only see the opposite—most young people who are trans try to present as cis for a period of time to deflect attention or to not stand out. It’s never been harder to be trans. While many things can be socially influenced, we know that gender identity cannot. Studies have shown that trans people report a long-standing knowledge of their gender identity—it often takes years before they disclose that identity outwardly. Gender conformity is the real social contagion at play, not trans identity.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Some people regret all types of medical care, whether it’s knee replacement surgery, spine surgery, or Accutane. With surgery and hormones, regret rates are less than 1 percent—among the lowest in all of medicine. But it’s also important to think about regret itself and what it means. People who express regret about surgery or hormones often say that it’s just too hard to be trans in an openly hostile society. For those people, it’s not health care at the root of regret—it’s housing discrimination, job loss, violence, and political targeting. Until we ensure the same dignities for trans people as we do for everyone else, we need to think about regret in dimensions and never use it as a reason to snuff out opportunities for care.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: You’d probably hold a door open for someone or make an effort to spell their name correctly in an email. You’d probably also expect others to get your pronouns right. Moreover, there are plenty of times when we simply can’t discern someone’s gender because they haven’t told us and we’ve never met them. We naturally revert to using gender-neutral pronouns in those cases. Keep that in mind and remember that something small can make someone else’s day.

Also, it’s totally normal to be nervous about getting this wrong. I get people’s pronouns wrong and it doesn’t feel great, but I own it and move on. I’ll usually say, “Hey, I got your pronouns wrong—next time, I’ll get it right!” It removes the awkwardness where the other person feels like they have to comfort the one making a mistake and it gets us back on track to whatever it was we were talking about.

Shane Diamond: I totally get it. It can feel unfamiliar to refer to someone using they/them pronouns, but we actually use those pronouns all the time for individuals. For example, if I found a backpack and was bringing it to a lost and found, I’d say, “Someone forgot their backpack.” I don’t know who forgot it, so using “their” acts as a singular person pronoun regardless of gender. It can take practice, but it’s not grammatically incorrect or even uncommon in our language. If using someone’s correct pronouns feels too clunky or too complicated, you can always skip pronouns and just use their name. But at the end of the day, using someone’s correct pronouns isn’t about you—it’s a way of showing respect for someone else.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Let’s get back to biology for a second: Ovaries and testicles both produce estrogen and testosterone. So this statement has some fundamental flaws. But let’s also think about what would happen if we applied this statement’s logic broadly: We’d cosign a lot of bad health care. We wouldn’t offer testosterone replacement therapy to cis men with low libido, we wouldn’t help ease cis women into menopause safely or respectfully, and we wouldn’t support people who lose those gonadal functions due to cancer. We wouldn’t be able to suppress life-threatening menstrual bleeding in people with bleeding disorders like hemophilia! I don’t think anyone wants that. If there’s a seat at the table of good health care for these cis people, then there’s a seat at the table for trans people, too.

Gillian Branstetter: The treatments transgender people access are no different from the treatments millions of cis people rely on for their health needs. And importantly, decisions about medical care should be kept between patients and their doctors—not politicians.

Gillian Branstetter: The good news is you don’t have to understand everything about a transgender person to know they’re as deserving of respect and equal treatment as everyone else, and that’s all most are asking for. Every day we interact with people of different races, religions, and backgrounds, and we don’t need to be an expert in their experience to trust them with their experience and treat them with the same dignity we would want for ourselves.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Imagine if you—as a woman—sprouted hair on your chest and had genitalia hanging outside of your body that made you feel completely unlike yourself. What if society would only treat you with dignity and respect if you wore masculine clothing? If you can step into that headspace for a second and experience a smidge of that discomfort, imagine feeling that every moment of your life. The fact that trans people can and do navigate gender dysphoria is beyond admirable. Let’s also reframe the concept of choice: People don’t want to be a gender—they are a gender. They choose whether to present themselves to society as that gender, and that choice is what gender-affirming care can facilitate.

Shane Diamond: We already have existing state and federal laws that protect people, specifically women, from predators, harassment, and assault. It’s already illegal to assault someone, regardless of gender or location. Despite fearmongering from anti-transgender voices, the truth is, transgender people are actually most at risk for being attacked in the restroom. Being that trans women are women and trans men are men, each should use the respective restroom that aligns with their gender. Ultimately, we’re all trying to do the same thing in restrooms—use a likely-under-serviced toilet and check our social media—regardless of our gender.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Wow, this is news to me! Every patient I’ve had who’s gotten any form of gender-affirming surgery has gone through years of deep introspection about what’s right for them. Once they’ve arrived there, they’ve jumped through hoop after hoop after hoop to connect with the right providers.

Shane Diamond: First, let’s talk about what this really is: medically necessary healthcare for trans and nonbinary people. (“Gender-reassignment surgery” is also an outdated phrase that’s not commonly used anymore.) Second, have you ever tried to schedule any kind of surgery in this country?! Nothing in health care happens quickly or easily, and that’s not even factoring in maneuvering insurance coverage. It’s also important to remember that there’s no single way to be transgender or nonbinary, and that not every trans or nonbinary person will choose—or be able to afford—a medical transition. These decisions, like all personal and private health care decisions, are best left to patients, their families if applicable, and their health care providers.

Shane Diamond: It is not true that people are claiming to be transgender when they are not. Given how difficult it is to navigate our complicated and tedious health care system and all the discrimination and challenges trans people face, no one is faking being transgender for any advantage—be it in sports or anywhere else.

Out of the millions of global competitive athletes, there are only a handful of transgender people competing at high-level sports, and—despite sensational media narratives—we’re not always winning! It is especially important in the context of youth sports that all kids—transgender and cisgender alike—have an opportunity to participate in sports with their friends to learn valuable, lifelong lessons through being part of a team...without being subjected to gender- and appearance-based discrimination.

Ultimately, these anti-trans actions harm all girls and women, punishing gender non-conformity of any kind and imposing rigid gender roles and expressions on everyone.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: The American Psychiatric Association doesn’t consider gender dysphoria to be a mental illness—it’s classified as a sexual health condition that arises from discordance between one’s physical body and sense of self. But let’s talk about the word “fix.” How? There is no alternative treatment for gender dysphoria beyond access to thoughtful, multidisciplinary gender-affirming medical care. It’s not right for everyone, but it’s right for many.

Shane Diamond: Being transgender and/or nonbinary is one of nature’s many variations—the way some people have blue eyes, brown eyes, or green eyes. Being transgender is an innate part of who someone is and there’s nothing anyone can do to change that. External forces attempting to change someone’s gender or sexual orientation are ineffective at best and extremely harmful at worst.

Transgender people are not the problem and are simply trying to live safely. The Trump administration has spent millions of dollars vilifying and discriminating against transgender people—why? The American public has many issues of importance, including the economy and climate change, and yet opponents of equality are wasting important resources and attention perpetuating hatred and divisiveness. If anything needs to be fixed, it’s our society's rigid rejection of anyone who is different.

Photos of experts are courtesy of subjects. Answers have been lightly condensed and edited for clarity.

#6911. Harry Shum Jr. Confirmed His ‘DWTS’ “Valerie” Performance Is a Direct Nod to ‘Glee’

The Dancing With the Stars semifinals kick off tonight, which means six couples are still in the running for the coveted Mirrorball trophy. I’m sorry to everyone else competing, but the night already belongs to Harry Shum Jr.

#6908. The One Record in Sports That Will Never Be Broken

In an age when numbers are paramount, a single statistic from hockey stands taller than the rest.

Back in my full-time sportswriting days—and, in fact, throughout my journalism career—I managed to disguise my fundamental innumeracy. Then suddenly, quite recently, numbers became paramount in sports. Baseball led the way, as it does in so many areas where boredom is involved. Now, thanks to gambling’s explosion in popularity, statistics have become one of the primary ways to be a fan.

In this era of analytics and the tyranny of numbers, there is one statistic that remains not only my favorite but that is likely never to fall, and it comes from, of all sports, hockey. It is this, which boggles the mind: Wayne Gretzky ended his career in 1999 with a total of 2,857 points, which includes goals and assists. Factor out the goals, and he’s still at 1,963, which is 42 more points than the player with the next-highest total, Jaromir Jagr, who is retired.

The first time I ever saw Gretzky play was during the 1988 Stanley Cup playoffs, when my Boston Bruins faced his Edmonton Oilers. These were the dynastic Oilers, loaded with talent: Mark Messier. Jari Kurri. Glenn Anderson. Grant Fuhr between the pipes. All of them Hall of Famers. Amid all this clamorous talent, Gretzky was a phantom. He had this strange and wonderful ability to vanish in the middle of the action and then, suddenly, appear in exactly the right spot at exactly the right time to make exactly the right play.

Gretzky will likely be the leading point scorer in NHL history for the next several decades. In fact, you have to go all the way down to seventh place on the list to find an active player, Sidney Crosby, who is 202 short of Gretzky’s 1,963 assist-only number. Another active player, Alex Ovechkin, is in tenth place, but he’s got one skate out the door already. The next active players on the list are Evgeni Malkin and Patrick Kane, at 23rd and 24th.

Like Gretzky himself, this statistic disappears among all the more important numbers until it suddenly pops out to amaze and astound. I like Sidney Crosby a great deal as a player, but I hope he never gets there.

Charles P Pierce is the author of four books, most recently Idiot America, and has been a working journalist since 1976.

#6907. Netflix Proved They Are the Masters of Heartland Stories at the Texas Book Festival

The Hunting Wives author May Cobb, bestselling author Katherine Center, and Virgin River showrunner Patrick Sean Smith got real about bringing your favorite cozy stories to life.

A packed theater in Austin, Texas, got together to see three of Netflix’s biggest stars right now—Virgin River showrunner Patrick Sean Smith, bestselling author Katherine Center, and The Hunting Wives author May Cobb—talk about their latest projects at the Texas Book Festival alongside the company’s Vice President of Drama Series, Cole Galvin. Not only is the streamer the home for each and every one of them, but their small-town stories filled with comfort and escapism have become major favorites for fans who simply cannot get enough of it all.

It’s no surprise, especially when you consider how much of an impact that the stories have had on viewers. According to the streamer, “since 2025, book adaptations have driven over 13 billion global views and represented nearly 20% of total view hours.”

May Cobb admits that The Hunting Wives’s success on Netflix—season 2 will be released on November 26—brought her novel to new levels of fandom she’s never seen before.

“Not to where it's in the zeitgeist. Not to where it's a cultural phenomenon, and now it has this incredible life of its own is absolutely staggering to me and I do give all credit to our creator, Rebecca Cutter, and to Netflix,” she admitted.

According to Christine Ball, EVP & Publisher at Berkley, “Originally published in 2021, May Cobb’s The Hunting Wives surged back into the spotlight following its Netflix premiere. In just one week, they have gone back to press twice on the trade paperback and saw a +5,000% increase across all formats in the same one-week period year over year.”

An external consultant noted that in U.S. book sales across physical, audio, and e-books, “lift in the 30 days after Netflix release against the same 30-day period the year prior, and found that, on average, Netflix adaptations increase book sales 10x.”

And it’s not only the authors who are feeling the impact. Even Spotify is getting a nice boost from it with My Life With the Walter Boys bringing a “1,000 percent increase in global listens and over 1,300 percent in global searches on Spotify,” to Ali Novak’s series following the season 1 premiere.

“It's so phenomenal and great because it brings people back to the books,” Katherine Center revealed to Cosmopolitan. Her book, The Bodyguard, will soon be a Netflix film titled Guarding Stars starring Jared Padelecki and Leighton Meester. The film had a sold-out screening at the Texas Book Festival where Katherine and Jared took the stage alongside producer Genevieve Padalecki. It also stars My Life With the Walter Boys star Noah LaLonde, bringing him to another book universe on the streamer.

“There are a lot of people who find out it's going to be a movie and want to read it before they see it,” Katherine continued. “Other people who see the movie, love the movie, and then go back and find the book. It's incredibly helpful.”

And while most streamers are also feeling major successes when it comes to adaptations, it’s clear that Netflix’s focus on comfort/heartland stories is paying off with, “over 20 comfort/heartland titles appearing in the Global top 10 with 6 of them reaching the #1 spot.”

Part of that is how the shows and movies that fill these slots get to make some folks who hardly see themselves on TV finally feel seen.

“I like being able to tell stories for the older generation, just because they're becoming a bit invisible on television,” Patrick Sean Smith said. “And having Tim Matheson and Annette O’Toole to write to is just insane, so that's never an opportunity that I want to squander. We fill out their lives with their peers and their friends and it feels fresh because you don't really see that much on television.”

May Cobb credits Taylor Sheridan’s impact on TV as the reason why we get to see so many of these stories get made away from major metropolitan cities like Los Angeles, New York, and Chicago. It also allowed them to push against the grain with what people might expect from a story based in the Lone Star State.

“What they did that's so interesting with this particular heartland story is they showed this like twistier, more subversive side of Texas that I don't know had been represented yet. I feel like it's not just Texas; it could be small town anywhere,” she noted.

As fans got to check out the Netflix Reading Lounge and pick up copies of their favorite books, it was clear that they all were reacting the same to each title. Options ranged from People We Meet on Vacation to the first volume of One Piece and The Magician’s Nephew, the basis of Greta Gerwig’s highly anticipated Narnia film that will be released in 2027. All books that can offer a bit of comfort in their new read. It helps that the lounge also included bookmark making and free coffee that was perfect to combat the sudden rain and cold that hit Austin during the festival weekend.

“People find me generally when they're going through something hard because they've heard that my books are funny, and they are looking to be lifted up a little bit,” Katherine said. “I hear, I found you after I had surgery. I found you when my mom was sick. I found you after I just lost somebody I loved.”

“There’s this concept in psychology called moral elevation that I think about a lot. It's this idea that when you witness somebody doing something kind for someone else, it gives you this kind of rising feeling in your body and it makes you want to be a better person. I think I'm sort of obsessed with how to create that feeling in a story.”

Patrick notes that he’s witnessed firsthand how these stories can affect even his own cast and crew members during the filming of Virgin River, showing the impact that they have even before fans get to see what happens.

“I've gone through a lot of things in my personal life that the show has helped me move through. I went through a divorce during the wedding season, which was really hard, but was incredibly cathartic,” he revealed. “When we did season five, we did an endometriosis storyline and there were a lot of women that came up and just said, ‘Thank you. I haven't seen this before.’ There's a symbiosis to it.”

And it’s that kind of universality that allows these stories to break down global barriers. According to Netflix, “Comfort/heartland titles have appeared in the Top 10 in 93 different countries. The book adaptations that made our Global Top 10 in 2026 came from 25 different countries, from Nordic crime fiction to an Indian romance novel to Mexican historical fiction.”

May also feels that impact in her home state, especially after the recent elections, noting that there were many memes that linked The Hunting Wives with Blexas’s success.

“No, it was the grassroots, and it was all the hard work,” she said. “I think it's cool how it's ahead of the curve and ahead of its time a little bit. Last week proved that Texas is more diverse in every way.”

It’s the universality that also allows Virgin River, which is set to become Netflix’s longest-running original scripted series thanks to its season 9 renewal, to continue on with such beloved fanfare with endless possibilities.

“I think it can go for as long as the fans continue to show up,” Patrick noted. “It's a place. It's a feel. It's a vibe that people like. We just want to make sure that we keep the storytelling fresh and let these characters grow up and move through life together.”

#6902. The Fall Essentials You Need According to 4 of the Most Stylish Guys We Know

Grails, staples, and everyday beaters abound.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fall has the singular ability to turn even the most Spartan man into a bit of a style savant. It’s like a wolf awakens the second that temperatures start to drop. Suddenly you see an Instagram post from the junior analyst below you; he must fancy himself a budding A$AP Rocky, because he’s rocking a statement scarf. Your brother-in-law is wearing a turtleneck. Your buddy is in white jeans. Every day, you zone out in dumb stakeholder meetings and fantasize about a new jacket or that pair of buttery-soft leather loafers you saw on a dude at the grocery store.

It’s because fall invites more—more layers, more textures. It can be freeing. No more worrying if that T-shirt will become a body scan of where and how you sweat; no more worrying if those pants will suffocate you. But ... it can be too much. How should you layer? How can you get funky for the holiday party without looking like an asshole? What brands are even worth buying?

We answer all those questions elsewhere in this magazine, but today we want you to take a breath. Keep it simple. How? By following some good examples.

A lot of the best-dressed guys right now are trimming down their wardrobes in favor of focusing on the basics. “This fall I’ve tried to trim the fat and make do with a much smaller wardrobe,” says actor, content creator, and friend of the magazine Albert Muzquiz—better known online by his handle @edgyalbert. “I only have a few shirts, a few pants, and a couple jackets—and I feel like I’m dressing better than ever.”

So instead of lobbing a bunch of meaningless trends, or -cores, at you, we decided to send you to some of the best-dressed men of the moment. You’ve heard enough from us; here’s what they’re feeling this fall.

Jake Mueser, founder of the eponymous bespoke suiting brand J. Mueser, has a handful of fall essentials. That cohort includes a cream turtleneck, which “you can throw on with anything,” as well as a classic tweed or herringbone blazer—same idea. They’re easy but plenty damn sexy.

Mueser also extols the virtues of a classic flannel suit—perhaps like the one he makes. “It works with everything from a denim shirt to a tie,” he says. “I like to break it up, wearing the jacket with vintage light-wash Wrangler cowboy cuts.” (A move we wholeheartedly endorse.)

J. Mueser Waverly Herringbone Virgin Wool-Tweed Blazer

J. Mueser Ivory Chunky Merino Roll-Neck Sweater

J. Mueser Charcoal Churchill Stripe Flannel Waverly Suit

There are a few reasons Drew Thomas can’t stop recommending merino sweaters—the thin, almost jersey sort—to his more than 500,000 TikTok followers. “You can get so much wear out of one,” he says. “You can layer it in the fall, wear it on its own on a slightly warmer day, or tie it around your shoulders. It works in the spring too, and even on a summer night when it gets a little cooler.”

Another seasonally versatile piece Thomas will stand ten toes down for is cream pants. In fact, Thomas likes to wear his all through fall. He keeps a few pairs on constant rotation. “I feel like the cream breaks up those darker fall colors and lets them stand out more,” he says.

When it comes to his next fall investment, Thomas is taking cues from the British sartorial invasion (“It never really went away, but it definitely feels like it’s having a moment again”) and looking to Barbour. “Something timeless and practical that I can wear for years and that only gets better with age,” he says. “I love the idea of owning something that develops character over time and could eventually be passed down to someone else. Those are the kinds of pieces I feel are worth investing in.”

J. Crew Relaxed-Fit Double-Pleated Corduroy Pant

“A down vest is my favorite fall garment,” Esquire contributor Christopher Fenimore told us. “It’s an easy outerwear piece to layer stuff under. I run hot, and I like having parts of my body exposed to the elements.”

We second that, but more predictably he highlights the entire category of shirting. Jackets and layers get a lot of shine, but this is the layer that, no matter how much temperatures fluctuate, will be on the skin. It’s a great time to get a few good oxford-cloth button-downs, polos, or beefier tees.

This time of year is also a great time to get clinical. “I assess things I have that might need updates, in terms of fit and silhouette or different fabrications depending on how I’d wear them,” he says. That can be rebuying your preferred undershirt or socks, or it could be a good time to jump on that thinner, more versatile merino sweater wave.

“Fall is the best time to break in a new pair of denim,” Muzquiz says. Wear it hard enough now and “it’ll be soft enough and possibly breathable enough for wear during the spring and parts of the summer.” No better time to get something esoteric.

When it comes to fall shoes, Muzquiz is into a classic six-inch lace-up boot. “There are so many bullshit brands out there that are trying to fool you with budget options,” he says. “Just get a pair of Red Wing Heritage if you’re starting out or Viberg if you really wanna flex.”

Muzquiz also finds himself taking part in the “buttery, delicate loafer movement” that’s been picking up steam as of late. The Yardee Moccasin loafers from Vinny’s are his pick of the bunch.

Viberg Navvy Boot - Brandy Hatch Grain Calf

#6897. Luke Grimes Didn’t Know If Marshals Would Work Without Kayce’s Wife

“He wants his little slice of heaven,” Grimes told Esquire when Yellowstone ended. “It’s that simple.”

Coming back home isn’t easy, especially for Luke Grimes. The Yellowstone actor spent all of the hit Taylor Sheridan drama as Kayce Dutton, a man trying to leave the past behind and move on from the sins of his father, family patriarch John Dutton (Kevin Costner). But when the western ended and Grimes had to say goodbye to the cast, crew, and his onscreen wife Kelsey Asbille (who played Monica Dutton), he really thought it was for the last time.

“Hopefully everyone can see that it was time,” Grimes told Esquire when Yellowstone ended. “There’s always talks of spin-offs. I’d do it [but] I just don’t understand how it would work once the story ends. Kayce wants to cowboy and be happy with his family. He doesn’t want to kill people anymore. He doesn’t want the weight of a huge mega ranch that isn’t sustainable in today’s times. He wants his little slice of heaven. It’s that simple.”

Paramount offered Grimes the option to return anyway on Marshals, a spin-off of Yellowstone that sees Kayce Dutton join the U.S. Marshals service in Montana. Reportedly, they won him over after pitching that they would take everything from Kayce so that he would be forced to start again. First on the chopping block? His wife, Monica.

“The thing I was most worried about doing Marshals was doing it without Kelsey,” Grimes told ScreenRant during an interview for season 2 last week. “I just couldn’t wrap my head around Kayce without Monica. That was the story. The rest of Yellowstone was about people fighting over land, and I just loved my wife. I had my own thing going on, and so I didn’t know what that would even look like.”

Eventually he came around to the idea. “Once I sort of, you know, could wrap my head around the fact that that was going to open up all these new storylines,” he admitted, “we just [had] to make sure we do honor [their life together] and don’t try to skip over it or pretend it didn’t happen.”

Marshals season 2 continues to air this fall, as Kayce Dutton explores a potential new relationship with fellow Marshal Andrea Cruz (Ash Santos). Sometime this season, former Yellowstone fan favorite Jimmy (Jefferson White) will also make an appearance.

#6895. “Reentry” Is Important Every Day of Your Relationship

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Every time you and your partner say “goodbye”—whether for the day or until your next hangout—you’re not exactly “exiting” the relationship. But, according to sex and relationship experts, you might still have to actively make a “reentry” when you come back together. In fact, it may be something you have to do every single day.

In a recent CNBC Make It article, psychologist Mark Travers says, “‘Reentry moments’ are the first minute or two when a couple comes back together after time apart.” And what you do with these moments can make all the difference when it comes to how you and your partner connect in your relationship.

When we’re deep in a relationship—whether because you’re a few years in or live together—we sometimes go into autopilot when we see each other again. We might get home after a long day of work and just continue with our evening routine while maneuvering around our partners, rather than with them. And while it’s totally okay to decompress after the day by yourself or even spend some evenings just doing our own thing, it’s still important to make a conscious effort to connect with our partners.

“Oftentimes, we are wearing so much protective armor to navigate the outside world and we don't even realize it,” Anastacia Sams, LMFT tells Cosmopolitan. “There is a certain way that we need to show up with our jobs, our friends, or in our other responsibilities, that we forget to lay our armor down and connect with our partner, human to human.”

LaTrease Nwosu, LPC explains that everyday reentry can look like giving each other a meaningful hug (one that lasts longer than 30 seconds), sharing a kiss, or—most effectively—asking them how their day was. It can look like just being curious about each other.

Reentry looks different for every couple depending on their core values and relationship goals. Sams explains that for couples that value good conversation, a 30-minute debrief about your day or something you learned is a great way to connect. For others, something as simple as an acknowledgement—“I miss you,” or “I’m looking forward to reconnecting later”—can make a partner feel secure and attended to.

“Small moments that show your consideration of one another add up, and reentry is the best time to do this,” Sams says.

If you and your partner already plan date nights and have uninterrupted quality time together, you might think that “reentry” isn’t going to make much of a difference. But a relationship is a continuous conversation. And sure, you might have a romantic date night once a month, but they shouldn’t feel like the only time you really feel close to your partner. “Reentry” can help maintain the connection every day of the relationship.

“When couples consistently skip these moments, it creates feelings of disconnection that lead to resentment,” Sams warns. “This is when couples start to feel they are just passing by each other throughout the day, or starting to feel like they are more roommates than romantic partners.”

She explains that over time, skipping these moments wear at the emotional safety of the relationship. Your partner may feel less inclined to share details about their life or take part in other forms of intimacy if this baseline of connection isn’t achieved.

But the good thing is that “reentry” isn’t the responsibility of one person in the relationship—both partners can work to initiate these moments so that it doesn’t always feel like only one person is trying to reconnect. Sams explains that “reentry” needs to be reciprocal in the sense that their needs to be mutual care, mutual respect, and mutual consideration between partners—but you don’t have to literally take turns checking in on each other.

“Either partner can initiate these moments of reconnection, and it is important for both partners to prioritize the intentionality of these moments without turning it into something that's transactional,” Sams says.

There are going to be days when you want to be alone, are having an argument with your partner, or are simply too overwhelmed to think about anything but your to-do list. Even in these times, I’d challenge you to keep “reentry” consistent. It doesn’t always have to be something that doesn’t honor your feelings, but being an attentive partner even when you’re tempted not to goes a very long way in relationships. It creates mutual respect and will encourage your partner to lead with consideration as well. No matter what’s going on in your life or as hectic as simply existing gets, remember that you chose to partner with this person for a reason. And by making an effort to “reenter” the relationship after time apart will do wonders for your connection.

#6894. The Best LED Mirrors for Your Bathroom

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It might sound dumb, but a smart option can improve your routine.

What is an LED mirror, and do you need one? These mirrors use an illuminated strip of light that you control to give more measured, even lighting than your bathroom overhead can. Some have a front light strip, some have backlighting, others have both. The best are equipped with antifog tech to make sure your mirror stays clear and functional during your entire post-shower routine. You’ll appreciate that when you’re shaving, and your wife won’t be able to go back to applying makeup without it. Because they use LEDs, they don’t use a significant amount of energy, and—so far—they are still low-tech enough to be largely free of AI.

So if they are so great, why doesn’t everyone have one of these fancy mirrors? The truth is it’s still hard to find a good one. The interest is there, but the market is currently flooded with $100 mirrors on Amazon that will work for a handful of months and then crap out on you. And the real good ones—the ones that merge tech and design—are expensive. These four run the gamut from affordable and convenient to luxe, but they all have one thing in common: You won’t be sending them back.

Everyone starts somewhere. And honestly the $200 Amazon option isn’t a terrible place to begin—it’s got over 3,000 reviews that mostly agree with me. Really this is everything that comes standard in a smart LED mirror: touch-controlled light settings, a defogging mode, and multiple power options (with a wall-plug option for apartments and more temporary dwellings).

The only real problem I can see is the design. The thick LED strip takes up a considerable amount of mirror real estate. Other, more thoughtful designs forgo this issue with thinner strips or backlighting. To be fair, you can turn that light off and just run with the backlight, but the chunky design of the mirror’s frame still shows.

If you want to just browse LED mirrors, Visual Comfort has a wide range of designer lighted mirrors. This one is not quite as striking, clearly, as the $2,000 options, but for $400 this is a trustworthy smart mirror that will have just what you want. What I especially like about the Mera is that you can separately adjust the brightness and color temperature.

For something really high-quality on all fronts, you’re going to have to pay top dollar. This is part of Visual Comfort’s Signature Collection, by designer Thomas O’Brien. What that really says is, from a design and craftsmanship angle, this is going to be a great mirror first—one that will last. Unfortunately, that does mean some of the tech comes secondary and not always in a way you might want.

But first off, the look. The gorgeous iron frame has a gilded finish that gives it a gold sheen. If you have the money to shell out for something fancier, this mirror is offered in nickel or (for the real high rollers) brass.

While it looks nice and has three brightness and color-temperature settings you can toggle between, it lacks any other “smart” features. Namely, there’s no defogging mode. And if you want this one installed, you’ll need to have an electrician do it.

The real problem with 99 percent of these mirrors is they just don’t look as natural or clean in your bathroom as a regular-ass mirror. This one from Seura actually does. When the backlight is not on, nobody will be able to tell the difference. When it is, you’ll reap all the benefits of a light that you can control.

These mirrors are a made-to-order, assembled-in-the-U.S.A. (Wisconsin, to be precise) type of deal. You can get them in all kinds of dimensions and, yes, even in a round shape, if that’s what you’re looking for. For dimming, you can have a control switch installed on the wall alongside the mirror or get the version that has touch controls.

#6892. Are Zen Hotels Now Putting Us to Sleep?

Rich, layered maximalism is the latest form of escapism—historically inspired decor spiked with creative contemporary flair. Get ready to Instagram every nook.

Above: One of the cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, CA.

On the top floor of a Gilded Age McKim, Mead & White mansion in New York, now reincarnated as the Fifth Avenue Hotel, Martin Brudnizki recently put the finishing touches on a two-bedroom suite dubbed the Flâneur Penthouse. With his hallmark exuberance, the Elle Decor A-List designer arrayed the space with distinctive flourishes: a spirited Scalamandré wallcovering trimmed in grosgrain ribbon, bespoke furnishings mixed with French Art Deco pieces, India-inspired inlaid nightstands, Japanese-style lamps, and Murano glass ceiling lights. Each element connects to an imagined narrative that shaped Brudnizki’s vision for the hotel: “this cinematic idea of the home of a flâneur who has traveled the world, has seen and acquired a lot, and put it together here,” the designer says. “It’s a multicultural journey.”

Hotels in this vein tend to be -owner-operated properties looking to carve out a niche. One could trace a baroque family tree that links mold-breaking 1980s boutique hotels like Ian Schrager and Philippe Starck’s Royalton in New York to Jacques Garcia’s interiors for the Hôtel Costes and L’Hotel in Paris to Ilse Crawford’s Ett Hem in Stockholm to Kelly Wearstler’s Proper Hotel in Austin.

Fulk’s hospitality design, like Brudnizki’s, embraces spaces inspired by history and storytelling, with an emphasis on artistry, material richness, and character. Both men’s work is rooted in tradition, with idiosyncratic twists that feel fresh. And they have plenty of kindred spirits working in a diversity of styles, including such designers as Lorenzo Castillo, Kit Kemp, and Laura Gonzalez. While far from a coherent movement, the sumptuous, high-impact aesthetics practiced by these designers just might be today’s most zeitgeisty current.

Take Fulk’s Cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, California, each one an elevated boho mashup of influences. “Sixties and ’70s rock and roll, and a sort of Laurel Canyon meets Northern California spirit,” explains the designer. “The cottages feel utterly like nowhere else.” And that, of course, is the point.

For his part, Brudnizki doesn’t care much for maximalism as a label. In the case of his glamorously moody interiors for the Broadwick Soho in London—shimmering surfaces, lavishly upholstered furnishings, a riot of pattern—everything began with an idea he describes as “Studio 54 meets your grandmother’s Soho townhouse, a disco granny kind of thing.”

Then there are the roughly dozen memorable properties created by Kit and Tim Kemp under their Firmdale Hotels group. The most recent is the Warren Street Hotel in New York, with the forthcoming Bedford Place Hotel slated to open next year in London next to the British Museum. “It’s about personality, a sense of arrival, and fun,” says Kit Kemp, who outfits each of her hotels with a more-is-more mix of her ebullient textiles and wallpapers in splashy colors, animated by art and craft.

Kimberley Cohen, the creative director behind Maisons Pariente, says her family’s small portfolio of hôtels de charme are about creating a residential-feeling experience that is also “something you have never seen, that you would not do in your own home.” She has worked with Brudnizki on Le Grand Mazarin in Paris, with Charles Zana on two hotels in the South of France, and with Pierre Yovanovitch on a Méribel chalet. Her latest project, opening this winter, is a Courchevel retreat named Saint Roch, with interiors by Friedmann & Versace that channel the chic romanticism of traditional dachas.

Next up for Brudnizki, meanwhile, is the Knox Hotel, set to open soon in Dallas, new territory for him. As for what one should expect of the decor, he sums it up as “100 percent unapologetic glamour. It won’t be boring.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6891. Princess Kate Is Bright in Pistachio Green and Cobalt Blue as She Arrives in Mumbai With Prince William

Princess Catherine and Prince William are starting their tour of India off strong. Today, the royal couple arrived in Mumbai for their three-day state visit—their first joint one in four years. And as photos show, the pair is ecstatic to be there.

Kate brought a colorful wardrobe for the mini trip with her husband. She made her first public appearance this morning in a fresh pistachio-green blazer from Veronica Beard, and then changed into a bright cobalt-blue dress from Roksanda.

She paired the blazer with a breezy white top, slim white trousers from McQueen, and black-and-beige flats with pearl-embellished buckles from Camilla Elphick for a fun game of street cricket. Dangly crystal earrings and a matching teardrop necklace completed the daytime look.

Then, to take a ride on Mumbai’s Metro and to attend the Earthshot Prize Impact Assembly, Kate changed into her vibrant Roksanda midi dress—the same one she wore to the Wimbledon men’s final in July 2025.

The design has a pretty V neckline. short, structured sleeves with a bow-like detail on one shoulder, a belted waist, and a flowy skirt.

The Princess of Wales styled the dress with chocolate-brown accents: suede Gianvito Rossi heels and the small Hudson bag from DeMellier.

Finally, she upped the glamour with blue and gold jewelry, including a pair of lapis lazuli-and-diamond drop earrings by Indian jewelry brand Amrapali—pieces which she wore to a charity gala in Mumbai during her 2016 tour of India.

William and Kate are in Mumbai to attend the sixth annual Earthshot Prize Summit, but during their visit will also take the time to carry out engagements relating to sustainability, innovation, and the relationship between India and the United Kingdom.

#6888. 11 Winter Colognes You Can Get for Under $100

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Smelling great doesn’t have to cost a fortune.

There’s never been a better time to get into personal fragrance. The cologne market is booming with frequent new launches from luxury fashion houses and perfumeries, yes—but it’s also filled with tons of great scents from more accessible and affordable brands. And with the temperatures finally dropping below 50°F in New York and Thanksgiving coming up next week, we’re officially in winter mode. That means getting our puffer coats out of storage and changing up our seasonal fragrance rotation.

So out with the fresh, aquatic, citrusy fragrances that belong on the beach and in with the warm, spicy, smoky scents that make the wintertime feel cozy. We’re talking cardamom and cloves, smoky tobacco and incense, sweet vanilla and smooth amber. Want to capture the scent of sitting by the fireplace and sipping a hot cocoa while wearing a cashmere sweater? These scents will do that—and at a fraction of the cost of a designer fragrance. We rounded up our picks for the winter that all come in at under $100, so you can save the big bucks for holiday shopping.

If you’ve checked out any of our other affordable-cologne roundups, you won’t be surprised to see Cremo at the top of our list here. We talk about the brand a lot, but it’s for a good reason: Cremo’s colognes are some of our favorites out there, and they literally cost under $25 each. Spice & Black Vanilla is our favorite spicy scent from the lineup, and the warm blend of cardamom, vanilla, tobacco, and woods is perfect for winter. Wear it for a cold-weather date night, a fancy dinner, or really any occasion.

Want a decent cologne mist you can easily buy on Amazon for just $13? Old Spice has been around for almost 90 years—yes, it may remind you of your dad, but it’s been around for so long because it’s a classic that just works. But shaking things up a bit, the brand released a new collection of cologne mists earlier this year, which includes this Cashmere & Vanilla Wood scent bursting with spices and powdery iris. For $13, you might as well order a few.

One of our favorite boozy colognes (and all-time favorite colognes in general) is Jazz Club from Maison Margiela’s Replica line. It’s one you’ve probably heard of and read about many times in our fragrance roundups, and since the 1 oz bottle is only $89, I just couldn’t leave it off this list. With rum, tobacco, and vanilla bean at the center, Jazz Club is the olfactory equivalent of sipping a cocktail in a smoke-filled bar. In other words, it’s the perfect sexy winter scent.

Gap recently introduced a new addition to its revitalized fragrance line (originally from the ’90s): Bliss. The gourmand EDP adds some creamy sweetness to the otherwise mostly fresh and earthy collection, and it came just in time for winter. It delights the senses with notes of butterscotch, cocoa bean, coconut milk, and vanilla bourbon, and it’s practically making my mouth water just thinking about it.

Huron has only five different scents, but it’s a true example of a brand that puts quality over quantity. I love each and every one of the brand’s EDPs, and while the marine Sea Salt + Driftwood and Original Citrus + Eucalyptus are both fresh and perfect for the summer, the Sandalwood + Black Pepper fragrance is definitely our winter choice. It’s warm, woody, and spicy, with some airy florals at the heart and rich amber and more woods at the base.

If you like the scent of Tom Ford’s Oud Wood but can’t get past that $300 price tag, we hear you. That’s why House of Twist made Wood No. 28; it’s not exactly Oud Wood, but it’s close enough. And for just $48, we’ll take it. The fragrance is a nice blend of nutty cardamom, oud wood, vanilla, and creamy sandalwood—a combination pretty much anyone can get behind.

Making the cut at $99 is Phlur’s newest fragrance: Heavy Cream. It’s a rich gourmand that’s balanced out by some bright citrus and airy florals at the top and heart of the fragrance, so it doesn’t get too overwhelmingly heavy. It definitely has the notes you’d expect of a fragrance inspired by heavy cream, like vanilla, amber, and creamy musks.

Fine’ry is another one of our go-to brands when it comes to affordable colognes we actually like. And this winter, our top choice from the lineup is Smoke & Leather, which smells like—you guessed it—smoky tobacco and rich leather with some woody undertones. It definitely brings the heat on a cold day.

Boy Smells is known for pairing traditionally masculine and feminine scent notes for truly unisex fragrances that anyone can enjoy. Hinoki Fantôme is a fan favorite thanks to its blend of spices, woods, and smoke, which are underscored by earthy oakmoss, orris, and patchouli. You really can’t put this fragrance in one category, because it spans so many. It’s a complex fragrance that really works, especially for the winter.

Earthy scents are chameleons that can work in any season, so it really just matters what they’re paired with. In the instance of Stetson’s Free Rein from its new Untamed collection, the pairing of earthy notes of wild verbena, sweetgrass, prairie moss, and patchouli with vetiver smoke and red amber makes it a warm, rich fragrance that adds some heat to your cold-weather outfit.

Florals are, generally speaking, made for spring and summer—but sometimes you get a gorgeous blend of florals with woods, musks, or amber, and it becomes perfect for wintertime. That’s exactly what Lafco has done with its Golden Morning scent: It’s got light floral notes of apple blossom, lavender, violet leaf, and rose, which all play nicely with a rich, sweet base of woods, honey, amber, and musks.

#6883. The 31 Best Gifts for the Snobbiest People You Know

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We found the stuff they don’t already own.

This is not meant to sound like a brag, but it inevitably will. Tons of luxurious products come across our desks; as such, I’ve developed especially discerning taste. I’ve tested more than my fair share of $500 colognes, $700 headphones, and $1,000 espresso machines over the years. Not that luxury is synonymous with price, but you know. While these occupational hazards might make me a challenge to shop for, they also give me a discerning eye for gifts that will actually impress my fellow snobs.

If they’re a coffee connoisseur who only drinks single-origin, always espresso, consider upgrading their at-home setup with Meraki’s dual-boiler espresso machine. For the audiophile whose vinyl collection rivals that of an actual record store, a Fluance turntable will do their records justice. And if their vanity would put a Saks to shame, an under-the-radar Creed fragrance and an award-winning red light mask are worthy additions.

The last thing they want is a bad interface or those ugly Alexa models. Thankfully, 2026 is the year Apple entered the home display market.

My brother has the most extensive at-home espresso setup I’ve ever seen, and he swears by this tamper. It’s spring-loaded to deliver consistent pressure, with an integrated lever for perfectly even pucks. If your espresso pulls aren’t flawless after this, time to look inward.

For a gift that will impress even the most particular of coffee connoisseurs, go with Meraki’s second-gen espresso machine. Featuring a built-in grinder, scales, and a dual-boiler system, it’s one of the most premium machines money can buy.

The ultimate snob’s wine opener. A needle and a bit of fancy pump work allow you to pour a glass without ever uncorking the bottle. Great for by-the-glass or sparing yourself any cork emergencies with older vintages.

Take it from someone who actually cooks—the home chef in your life doesn’t want gimmicky gadgets. Instead, gift them practical, high-quality cookware they’ll actually use, like this editor favorite stainless steel frying pan from All-Clad’s D3 line.

For the foodie who worships at the altar of Bourdain, consider gifting them his exact chef’s knife of choice. The Japanese-made Global G-2 has sleek all-metal construction, with a textured handle for a nonslip grip. What’s more, it can be custom-engraved with their name or initials.

For the giftee who’s always complaining that their matcha isn’t the right color (guilty), consider this tea kit. It has everything they need for the perfect cup at home, including a sifter, measuring spoon, whisk, and ceremonial-grade matcha.

Dubbed our top pick for audiophiles, this turntable has it all: consistently great audio quality, a built-in speed control mechanism, and an attractive wood finish.

Aesthetics and audio quality don’t always go hand-in-hand for headphones, which is precisely why this Bang & Olufsen pair is a standout. Our editors named it the best pair of noise-canceling headphones for sound quality, and it’s easily the most attractive model on the list.

If they’ve ever slept at one of the world’s best hotels, they have slept on Frette sheets and worn a Frette robe. Give them that luxury at home.

Every chess player I’ve ever met takes the game extremely seriously, and this is a set they’d be proud to display on their coffee table. Bonus: You can monogram it for a personal touch.

The best home gifts not only spark conversation, but also serve a purpose in their space. Pair this Greek mythology-inspired incense holder with some great-smelling incense (I’m partial to Le Labo), and you have an infallible gift for the artsy types on your list.

All the interior design obsessives I know in Brooklyn (myself included) are obsessed with this lighting brand, which draws from Bauhaus and mid-century modern influences. This retro, saucer-shaped table lamp is a foolproof pick for the giftee who wouldn’t be caught dead turning on “the big light.”

I have to hand it to the cinephiles here: Dolby Atmos is truly superior for watching movies (sorry, films) at home. This Sonos soundbar offers unmatched 3D surround-sound in a sleek package.

A candle is only “impersonal” if it’s ugly or smells bad. Loewe’s incense candle is beautiful, and it smells divine. No snob will ever turn one down.

Sure, Aventus might be Creed’s most popular cologne, but the fragrance snob you know doesn’t want to smell like every other guy. Instead, opt for one of the brand’s under-the-radar scents, like Bois du Portugal. Its notes of lavender, sandalwood, and cedar offer an aromatic scent reminiscent of a vintage barbershop.

Ah, the status hand soap. A gift that works for virtually anyone, especially those with discerning taste. This pick from Nécessaire somehow feels less sceney than other options on the market, and it’s infused with skin-softening ingredients like vitamin B3 and linoleic acid.

As a fragrance expert who tests new scents for a living, few earn me as many compliments as Althair from Parfums de Marly. It’s a grown-up vanilla, with notes of orange blossom, cinnamon, and a dose of smoky guaiac wood to keep it from going saccharine. Although it’s technically from the men’s line, its not-too-sweet gourmand scent profile is truly genderless.

The iRestore red light face mask just won a 2026 Gadget Award. Enough said.

Their parched, damaged strands deserve nothing but the best; in this case, it’s Kérastase’s Oléo-Relax hair mask. It’s got a ton of hydrating ingredients—rose oil, coconut oil, and shorea oil—to replenish dry hair. Not to mention, it smells damn good.

#6878. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Will Air Finale Live on West Coast for First Time Ever

Dancing With the Stars is making a major change heading into the finale next Tuesday! The show will air its three-hour finale live on both the East Coast and the West Coast, instead of only going live on the East Coast and having West Coast viewers watch the show late and risk being spoiled.

According to Deadline, the Dancing With the Stars finale will air Tuesday, November 24, at 8 p.m. ET and 5 p.m. PT. It will then re-air at 8 p.m. PT for everyone on the West Coast unable to catch the live viewing.

Of course, this means that an enormous amount of viewers on the West Coast will be able to vote live in the finale for the first time ever, which could definitely influence the outcome of the show!

So, why doesn’t Dancing With the Stars air live on both the West and East Coasts every week? Showrunner Conrad Green told The Hollywood Reporter that it conflicts with local news on the West Coast. As he put it, “local news and the coveted primetime programming slots” get in the way of a live show—but there’s also an issue with rights.

“It’s a big issue for [affiliates] is if everyone starts watching the show at 5 p.m. when it airs,” he said. “If you’re in L.A. and you watch at 5 p.m. when it airs [at 8 p.m.] on the East Coast, then it’s going to massively affect the people who will watch the show when it comes over to these different territories and those markets. The affiliates are hugely important in the bigger picture for the overall economic structure of the show and for the history of ABC and all of those things.”

Clearly ABC made an exception for the finale!

#6876. Robert Pattinson Is the James Bond Millennials Deserve

The 40-year-old actor has been chosen to become the seventh James Bond in film, following in the footsteps of Daniel Craig

The news is finally out: Robert Pattinson is the next James Bond. After weeks of speculation and an almost algorithmic audition process that played out on our social feeds, Pattinson has triumphed over a list of peers that reportedly included Nicholas Hoult, Callum Turner, and Josh O’Connor.

For the last few weeks, I’ve watched the rolling news coverage of the casting process with trepidation, because the concept of a machismo-fueled, womanizing killing machine feels tired and outdated in 2026. But that’s exactly why the choice of Pattinson made me breathe a sigh of relief. Since breaking out in the 2000s,Pattinson’s career has been defined by his willingness to subvert traditional masculine archetypes, crafting them instead into slightly twisted leading-man roles. From his starring role in 2022’s The Batman to the 2026 rom-com The Drama and most recently the disturbing Oscar-tipped biopic Primetime, he has often looked for ways to subvert our expectations of the classic leading man. If there’s one actor who just might be able to make 007 feel aspirational and relevant again, it’s him.

From the beginning, Pattinson, who first emerged as a teen icon in the Twilight movies, has represented a counter-argument to traditional, protein-based masculinity. “Team Edward” and “Team Jacob,” the franchise’s central romantic rivalry (and marketing ploy), offered two very different visions of male desirability. Jacob, played by Taylor Lautner, was part man, part wolf, and was muscular, physically imposing, and (thankfully) almost always shirtless, which today draws more of a visual parallel with the sort of hypermasculinity that has flourished in online gym-bro culture and the so-called “manosphere.” And then there was Edward, Pattinson’s pale, brooding vampire who was more sensitive and emotionally tortured. With the selection of a new Bond, it feels like we’re once again seeing this masculine power struggle play out—and again, it’s Pattinson who has come out on top.

In the packed field of chiseled, handsome, talented actors who were vying for this role, whoever snagged it was always going to have his work cut out. As an archetype, 007 is a relic of generations gone by. The flashy gadgets, the signature martini, the expensive cars, and the ability to charm almost any woman belong to a very particular, nostalgic vision of masculinity. Through a more contemporary lens, Bond is the ultimate masculine drag act: an exaggerated, prop-heavy performance that is just as likely to be viewed as regressive and cliché.

We see the same trend in Bond’s dwindling romantic appeal. In our grandparents’ generation, when the character was at peak aspiration, a man arriving in a nice suit, carrying flowers, and driving a flashy car was considered a catch. But today, the dynamics have changed. I recently read The Project, the debut novel by British Vogue columnist Annie Lord, where she makes the observation that many of the most common dating “icks” now stem from men trying too hard: putting themselves out there too much, making an overt display of their interest, or seeming too confident. Lord notes that we’ve become accustomed to viewing these qualities as undesirable or embarrassing, which draws clear parallels with Bond, whose entire appeal has rested on this sort of performance. The spy’s extravagance and his carefully cultivated image were once what made him attractive, but can you imagine going on a date with a guy who seriously asks for his martini to be “shaken, not stirred”? You’d probably tell your group chat about it and endlessly mock him.

Looking more widely, there is also the question of whether the Bond franchise’s particular type of escapism can still work in the fraught world we’re currently living in. The franchise once offered a fantastical vision of evil, with megalomaniacs plotting world domination in ways that felt almost comically removed from reality. (Remember Bond’s “invisible” car in 2002’s Die Another Day?) But now, the real world feels uncomfortably close to that fantasy. Billionaires wield the kind of influence once reserved for nation-states, and the increasingly overt relationship between money and power makes it harder to distinguish the Bond villain caricature from what we’re encountering in day-to-day life.

Part of the reason Pattinson landed this role, I suspect, is that he already has experience in navigating nostalgic expectations and present-day anxieties. In The Batman, for example, he proved his ability to bring new dimensions to an old-school character with his tortured take on Bruce Wayne. And as a versatile actor who can move between action blockbusters, rom-coms, and critically acclaimed projects like Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey, he has the star power and the credibility to give Bond a millennial makeover.

It’s worth saying that during Daniel Craig’s tenure as Bond, considerable effort was made to turn Bond into a deeper, more considered character. Craig’s films explored Bond’s childhood trauma and alcohol dependence, and they also introduced female characters who were more than just a sidekick or a throwaway romantic interest. But in the five years since Craig departed, a loud and unrepentant misogyny has taken hold in corners of the internet. And from the Epstein files to the Cornell University rape allegations and, most recently, the conviction of Russell Brand, so many stories come back to the central issue of toxic masculinity.

The wider conversation about male behavior has made casting the next Bond feel strangely high-stakes, which is probably why every screen test seemed to be leaked and endlessly dissected online. And it’s also why I’m relieved that they’ve picked an actor who might be able to make Bond a little more strange. Pattinson presents the opportunity to take Bond in a different direction—one that finally frees the character from the fantasies of the past.

#6873. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Votes Are Reportedly “Significantly Lower” Than Last Season

Buuut the show is more popular than ever!

Dancing With the Stars might be airing one of its most-watched seasons yet, but apparently audience participation isn’t as high as usual. Despite fans tuning into the show week after week, turns out not that many people are picking up their phones to actually vote—or, at least, not as many as last season.

“The amount of votes coming in right now is significantly lower than this time last season because the Alix [Earle] vs Robert [Irwin] face-off sparked a ton of participation,” a source tells The Sun. “Producers are somewhat seeing that with Amber and Ezra and Tatyana. But there’s no one garnering that level of response this season and it shows with the numbers.”

Despite the low voting, Deadline reports that Dancing With the Stars’ opening episode saw a 24 percent increase from last season’s premiere. And fans even crashed the voting site! Meanwhile, Super Bowl Night saw the show get a “nine-year ratings high among adults 18-49” with 6.59 million live/same-day viewers.

Last year’s season 34 finale brought in 72 million votes (more than double the 32 million in season 33), and while votes are allegedly down this season, it’s possible next week could change things. Mostly because—for the first time ever!—ABC is airing Dancing With the Stars live on both the East Coast and the West Coast, meaning West Coast viewers will be able to watch the show as it airs and vote in real time. This should inevitably get the numbers up, so stay tuned…and remember to vote for your favorites during the finale next week at 8 p.m. ET!

#6870. The Masculinity Defenders Are Already Whining About Robert Pattinson’s James Bond

Every incel with a YouTube account thinks R-Patz isn’t manly enough to be the face of a film franchise that contains a movie called Octopussy.

After an exhaustive and exhausting years-long casting process, Robert Pattinson has been announced as the new James Bond, and right on cue, the masculinity defenders are being super normal about it.

I thought the big takeaway was that we have the first James Bond with a cute little social media nickname (which led me to wonder whether we’d have been calling Pierce Brosnan “P-Broz” if there had been a Twitter in 1995). But nope: the deal is that R-Patz is not manly enough to be the face of a film franchise that contains a movie called Octopussy.

On a YouTube show called Dan Wootton: Outspoken, special guest and self-described “right-wing comedian” Leo Kearse said Pattinson is “Not a man! He doesn’t have a hairy chest, no drink problem, he’s never bought a fridge.” Okay! Point by point: 1) Daniel Craig was smooth as a baby seal. 2) Watch The Lighthouse if you think R-Patz can’t convincingly put them away. 3) What?

Kearse, ignorant and undeterred as a real man ought to be, continues: “He probably still sleeps in a car shaped like a Ferrari, with Powerpuff Girls sheets.” Also he and Wootton both seem to be calling him “Robert Patterson” throughout. Great stuff. I mean this, Leo Kearse: you are the funniest right-wing comedian I’ve ever seen.

Interesting, except that the next Bond movie will be written by British screenwriter Steven Knight, who wrote the ultra-violent Eastern Promises, created the epic gangster series Peaky Blinders, and co-created Who Wants to Be a Millionaire?, which doesn’t have anything to do with James Bond or the broader masculinity conversation per se, but goddamn what a career.

In The Atlantic, Tom Nichols complained that “Pattinson lacks the manly gravitas …Pattinson is sensitive; he broods; he’s conflicted.” He concludes, “I imagine that when the mad criminal offers him a mint julep, Pattinson will scowl and explain that alcohol and sugar are bad for you.” Listen: if there’s anything more masculine than imagining a scenario and then getting angry at it, it’s fretting endlessly about what is and is not masculine.

Right under a piece whining that “the left-wing bias of The Grammys is on full display” (because Michelle Obama got a Best Audiobook nomination and Jesse Welles’s “No Kings” is nominated for Best Americana Roots Recording), the Blaze wrote a pretty straightforward news post about the casting, and although it’s journalistic malpractice to quote any comments section to prove any larger societal point, when you get comments like…

“Is he still licensed to kill, or will he be an unarmed social worker with a handbook on de-escalation negotiation?”

On social media and comments sections and whatever that Dan Wootton show is, exhibit A for the case against Robert Pattinson’s masculinity is a 2023 Paris Fashion Week red carpet look: Pattinson in a fur jacket and a skirt. I don’t know, I think a man ought to defy conventions, go his own way, take some risks. Plus, the dude can rock a skirt. Don’t hate him because he’s beautiful.

So Pattinson is too fey to play Bond, and too famous from other iconic roles besides. This is, of course, the exact criticism leveled at Roger Moore in 1972 when he took the role over: that he was too lithe to compete with the hairy-shouldered thickness of Sean Connery, and that he was already too well-known for having played a similar role in The Saint. (And for having done it on television; “TV Star” was an epithet until right around The Sopranos.) Timothy Dalton was dinged for being too Shakespearean to take over for Moore. Pierce Brosnan had already played a parody of a Bond type on Remington Steele, so how could he compete with the gravitas of Dalton? Daniel Craig was criticized for being too beefy to take over for Brosnan. You can’t win.

The good news is that as much whining as there is about Pattinson and his lack of masculinity, there’s a lot less of it than there would have been even a few months ago. The “not man enough” chorus is singing, but they’re undeniably a little bit muted. Whether it has anything to do with Pete Hegseth and Markwayne Mullin and Sean Duffy and the rest of our government’s drag king grotesques of manliness publicly tripping on their dicks and compromising our basic security in the process I can’t say definitively, but it sure feels like it.

Among the many YouTube reaction videos, I came upon one that I am absolutely not going to watch, but whose title distills the argument down to its essence almost too perfectly.

There it is. I wanted someone else. The new thing is bad. The whole argument, like so many of the arguments against the evolution of masculinity, boils down to “things aren’t the way they were when I was younger, and I’m angry about it.” It’s the credo of the dull, the philosophy of the frightened, the logline of at least three Tim Allen sitcoms. Move on. Grow up. Let R-Patz cook.

I’ll meet you back here when we’re furious about Taylor Swift doing the theme song.
